The Derived Auslander–Iyama Correspondence II: Bimodule Calabi–Yau Structures
Gustavo Jasso, Fernando Muro

Abstract
. Let d d be a positive integer. In a previous article we established a bijective correspondence between the following classes of objects, considered up to the appropriate notion of equivalence: differential graded algebras with finite-dimensional 0 0 -th cohomology such that the canonical generator of their perfect derived category is a basic d ​ ℤ d\mathbb{Z} -cluster tilting object, and basic Frobenius algebras that are twisted ( d + 2 ) (d+2) -periodic as bimodules. For d = 1 d=1 this correspondence specialises to previous work of the second-named author on algebraic triangulated categories of finite type. In this article, we prove a variant of our general correspondence for bimodule right Calabi–Yau dg algebras. A novel ingredient is a new cohomology theory which contains obstructions to the existence and uniqueness of minimal A ∞ A_{\infty} -bimodule structures on a graded bimodule.

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Calabi–Yau triangulated categories

We work over a field
\mathbf{k}
. Let
\mathcal{T}
be an algebraic
1
1

1

A triangulated
category is
algebraic
if it is equivalent to the homotopy category of a
pre-triangulated dg category
[
Kel06
, Section 3.6]
.
triangulated category
with finite-dimensional morphism spaces and split idempotents. Following
Kontsevich
[
Kon98
]
, the triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
is

n
-Calabi–Yau
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, if there exist natural isomorphisms

\mathcal{T}(y,x[n])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D\mathcal{T}(x,y),\qquad{x,y\in\mathcal{T}},

where
V\mapsto DV
denotes the passage to the linear dual, that exhibit the

n
-fold power of the shift functor as a (graded) Serre functor in the sense
of
[
BK89
]
.
2
2

2

See
[
Kel08
, Section 2.6]
and Van den
Bergh’s
[
Boc08
, Appendix A]
for the precise definition of a graded Serre
functor as well as a discussion of sign issues.
The bounded derived category
of coherent sheaves on an
n
-dimensional Calabi–Yau smooth projective variety
is an
n
-Calabi–Yau triangulated category, hence the terminology. Not least
due to Kontsevich’s celebrated Homological Symmetry Conjecture
[
Kon95
]
,
Calabi–Yau triangulated categories also play an important role in symplectic
geometry. In representation theory of quivers and algebras, prime examples of
Calabi–Yau triangulated categories are (higher) cluster
categories
[
Bua+06
,
Kel05
,
Ami09
,
Guo11
,
FKQ24
]
and stable categories of
suitable self-injective algebras including preprojective algebras of Dynkin
quivers and higher-dimensional generalisations thereof
[
AR96
,
CB00
,
IO13
]
.
We refer the reader to the survey articles
[
Kel08
,
Kel10
]
for further
context and examples.

Recall that a differential graded (=dg) algebra
\Gamma
is
homologically
smooth
if it is compact as a dg
\Gamma
-bimodule. Following Kontsevich and
Ginzburg
[
Gin06
]
, such a dg algebra
\Gamma
is
bimodule left

n
-Calabi–Yau
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, if there exists an isomorphism

\mathbb{R}\!\operatorname{Hom}_{\Gamma^{e}}\!\left(\Gamma,\Gamma^{e}\right)\cong\Gamma[-n],\qquad\Gamma^{e}\coloneqq\Gamma\otimes\Gamma^{\mathrm{op}},

in the derived category of dg
\Gamma
-bimodules, and it is a general fact that
the finite-dimensional (=perfectly valued) derived category of
\Gamma
, that is
the full subcategory of the unbounded derived category
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{}}\!\left(\Gamma\right)
spanned by
the dg
\Gamma
-modules whose cohomology has finite total dimension, is an

n
-Calabi–Yau triangulated category in this case
[
Kel08
, Lemma 4.1]
. For
example, the dg algebra
C_{*}(\Omega X)
of chains on the loop space of an

n
-dimensional compact orientable manifold is bimodule left

n
-Calabi–Yau
[
Lur09
,
CG15
]
(see also
[
BD19
, Theorem 5.4]
). More
generally, every homologically smooth dg algebra
B
admits a (left)

n
-Calabi–Yau completion
\mathbf{\Pi}_{n}(B)
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, that, as the name
suggests, has the bimodule left
n
-Calabi–Yau
property
[
Kel11
,
Yeu16
,
BCS24
]
. Keller’s Calabi–Yau completion
simultaneously generalises the construction of the preprojective algebra of a
quiver
[
GP79
,
BGL87
]
and that of the Ginzburg dg algebra of a quiver with
potential
[
Gin06
]
if one takes into account deformations. For example, up
to quasi-isomorphism, preprojective algebras of extended Dynkin quivers arise as

2
-Calabi–Yau completions, and there is a suitable generalisation of this
important fact to higher dimensions
[
HIO14
]
. It is also worth mentioning
that (deformed) Calabi–Yau completions arise also in symplectic geometry, see
for example
[
EL17
,
LU24
]
. In algebro-geometric terms, the passage to the
Calabi–Yau completion corresponds to the passage from a smooth algebraic
variety to the total space of its (shifted) cotangent bundle
[
BCS24
]
. In

Section

1.3
we recall some examples that motivate our work in which
Calabi–Yau completions play a prominent role.

In this article, a sequel to
[
JKM22
]
, we investigate the question of how
the main theorem therein interacts with bimodule Calabi–Yau properties. We are
concerned with the following alternative notion of a Calabi–Yau dg algebra,
which is also due to Kontsevich
[
Kon93
]
(see also
[
KS06
,
BD19
]
). Let

A
be a dg algebra whose cohomology is degree-wise finite-dimensional. We say
that
A
is
bimodule right
n
-Calabi–Yau
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, if there exists an
isomorphism

A[n]\cong DA,\qquad DA\coloneqq\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(A,\mathbf{k}\right),

in the derived category of dg
A
-bimodules, where
DA
is the (differential
graded) linear dual of the diagonal
A
-bimodule. In this case, the perfect
derived category
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)
, that is the full subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{}}\!\left(A\right)

spanned by the compact objects, is
n
-Calabi–Yau as a triangulated category.
Under a suitable refinement, the bimodule right Calabi–Yau property can be
regarded as being Koszul dual to the bimodule left Calabi–Yau property, see for
example
[
KW21
, Proposition 10.4]
and the more recent
[
BCL25
]
. Under
Koszul duality, Keller’s Calabi–Yau completion of a homologically smooth dg
algebra corresponds to Segal’s cyclic completion of dg algebras with
finite-dimensional cohomology
[
Seg08
]
, which can be understood as a derived
analogue of the passage from a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
to its
trivial extension
{\Lambda\ltimes D\Lambda}
. This ties with the fact that the
bimodule right Calabi--Yau property can be understood as a derived version of
the notion of a symmetric algebra.
3
3

3

Recall that a (necessarily
finite-dimensional) algebra is
symmetric
if
\Lambda\cong D\Lambda
as
a
\Lambda
-bimodule.
In representation theory of algebras, cyclic
completions are central to the construction of higher cluster categories of
finite-dimensional algebras of finite global dimension
[
Kel05
,
Ami09
,
Guo11
]
.
Finally, we mention that bimodule right Calabi–Yau algebras play a central role
in Kontsevich and Soibelman’s approach to Donaldson–Thomas
invariants
[
KS08
]
and are also abundant in symplectic geometry in the form
of certain flavours of Fukaya categories
[
Fuk10
]
(see also
[
CL11
]
).

1.2.
Main results

The central concept that is investigated (implicitly) in this article is that of
a cluster tilting object, whose definition is due to Iyama
[
Iya07a
]
in the
abelian setting and Iyama and Yoshino
[
IY08
]
in the triangulated setting,
see also
[
GKO13
,
IJ17
]
for the ‘periodic’ variant below. For
{d=2}
,
cluster tilting objects play a fundamental role in the additive categorification
programme of Fomin–Zelevinsky’s cluster algebras
[
Bua+06
,
Ami09
,
Kel08a
]

and, in general, are central to Iyama’s higher-dimensional version of
Auslander–Reiten Theory
[
Iya07a
,
Iya07
,
Iya11
]
. We refer the reader to the
introduction to our previous article
[
JKM22
]
for a more thorough discussion
of this concept and for motivation that is relevant to this article as well.

Definition

(Iyama–Yoshino)
.

Let
d\geq 1
. A (basic) object
C\in\mathcal{T}
is

d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting
4
4

4

If the triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
is
not necessarily
\operatorname{Hom}
-finite, it is necessary to impose the
additional condition that the subcategory
\operatorname{add}(A)\subseteq\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)
is
functorially finite in the sense of
[
AS80
]
.
if

\forall i\not\in d\mathbb{Z},\qquad\mathcal{T}(C,C[i])=0,

and the following equalities of full subcategories of
\mathcal{T}
hold:

\displaystyle\operatorname{add}(C)

\displaystyle=\left\{X\in\mathcal{T}\,\middle|\,\forall 0<i<d,\ \mathcal{T}(X,C[i])=0\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{Y\in\mathcal{T}\,\middle|\,\forall 0<i<d,\ \mathcal{T}(C,Y[i])=0\right\},

where
\operatorname{add}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{T}
denotes the closure of
C
under the formation of
finite direct sums and retracts in
\mathcal{T}
. Under the presence of the second
condition, the first vanishing condition is equivalent to the existence of an
isomorphism
C\cong C[d]
in
\mathcal{T}
.

For example, if
d=1
then a
1\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object is simply an additive
generator of
\mathcal{T}
. By definition, a triangulated category
\mathcal{T}
is

additively finite
if and only if it admits an additive generator, thus the
existence of a
1\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object in
\mathcal{T}
places us precisely in the
setup of the second-named author’s
[
Mur22
]
. More generally, although it is
not obvious from the definition, a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object must generate

\mathcal{T}
as a triangulated category with split idempotents
[
IY08
, Theorem 3.1]
.
Since the ambient category
\mathcal{T}
is assumed to be algebraic, the presence of a

d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object
C\in\mathcal{T}
therefore implies the existence of a dg
algebra
A
and an equivalence of triangulated categories
[
Kel94
]

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{T},\qquad A\longmapsto C.

The above equivalence induces an isomorphism of graded algebras

\textstyle\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\cong\bigoplus_{di\in d\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{T}(C,C[di]),\qquad g*f\coloneqq g[dj]\circ f,\qquad|f|=dj;

since the object
C\in\mathcal{T}
is
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting, these graded algebras are
‘
d
-sparse’ in the sense that they are concentrated in degrees
d\mathbb{Z}
. The main
results in our previous works,
[
Mur20
]
for
d=1
and

[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
in general, show that—remarkably—the dg algebra
A

above is uniquely determined up to quasi-isomorphism by the finite-dimensional
algebra
\operatorname{H}^{0}\!\left(A\right)\cong\mathcal{T}(C,C)
and the
\operatorname{H}^{0}\!\left(A\right)
-bimodule

\operatorname{H}^{-d}\!\left(A\right)\cong\mathcal{T}(C,C[-d])
. Therefore it is natural to ask whether the property
of the dg algebra
A
being bimodule right Calabi–Yau can be detected at the
level of the graded algebra
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
as well. For example, an apparent necessary
condition is the existence of an isomorphism of graded

A
-bimodules
5
5

5

Here and thought the article, we write
V\mapsto V(1)

for the shift of a graded vector space, and reserve the notation
V\mapsto V[1]
for cochain complexes/differential graded vector spaces.

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),

which in cohomological degree
0
implies the existence of an isomorphism of
\operatorname{H}^{0}\!\left(A\right)
-bimodules

\mathcal{T}(C,C[n])=\operatorname{H}^{n}\!\left(A\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D\operatorname{H}^{0}\!\left(A\right)=D\mathcal{T}(C,C).

Before stating the first main result in this article, we remind the reader of
the Hochschild cohomology

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)=\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathbf{\Lambda},\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)\coloneqq\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathbf{\Lambda}^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathbf{\Lambda},\mathbf{\Lambda}\right),

defined for any graded algebra
\bf\Lambda
.

Theorem C
.

Let
\mathbf{k}
be a perfect field. Fix integers
d\geq 1
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and
set
6
6

6

The dg algebras in
Theorem

C
have their
cohomology concentrated in degrees
d\mathbb{Z}
, and hence can only have the
bimodule right
n
-Calabi–Yau property for
n\in d\mathbb{Z}
.

n\coloneqq md
.
Let
A
be a dg algebra such that
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
is degree-wise
finite-dimensional
7
7

7

Equivalently, the prefect derived category

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)
is
\operatorname{Hom}
-finite.
and whose
0
-th cohomology
is a basic finite-dimensional algebra. Suppose
A\in\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)
is a

d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object. Then, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

The dg algebra
A
is bimodule right

n
-Calabi–Yau.

(2)

There exists an isomorphism of graded

A
-bimodules

\varphi\colon\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)

and

0=\Delta(\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{A}}\}\!\!\})\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+1,-d}\!\left(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\right)

where
\Delta=\Delta_{\varphi}
is the Batalin–Vilkoviski operator (
Definition

4.76
)
and
\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{A}}\}\!\!\}
is the univeral Massey product of length
d+2
of

A
(
Definition

6.24
).

The universal Massey product of length
d+2
is an invariant associated to any
dg algebra with
d
-sparse cohomology, and it also plays a crucial role in the
proofs of the main theorems in
[
Mur22
]
and of
[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
. The
vanishing condition in
Theorem

C
(
2
)
is of obstruction-theoretic nature. The Batalin–Vilkoviski (=BV) operator is
obtained, as explained in
[
Tra08a
]
, by dualising Connes’ boundary operator

B
on the Hochschild homology of
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
by means of the graded bimodule
isomorphism
\varphi
. The BV operator has been considered in relation to
Calabi–Yau algebras already in Ginzburg’s seminal preprint
[
Gin06
]
(see
also
[
CBEG07
]
) and has been further investigated in this and other closely
related contexts by several authors, see for
example
[
Lam10
,
Che+21
,
CYZ16
,
LZZ16
,
Vol16
]
among many others. Furthermore,
the well-known relationship between the
B
operator and the ISB sequence of
Connes relates the BV operator to cyclic and negative cyclic homology, see for
example
[
CYZ16
, Section 8]
, and the latter cohomology theories are known to
control the deformation theory of Calabi–Yau algebras
[
PS95
,
VB18
]
.
From this point of view, the vanishing condition in

Theorem

C
(
2
) is very natural, and
is in fact satisfied by every bimodule right Calabi–Yau dg algebra with

d
-sparse cohomology (
Proposition

6.27
). Notwithstanding, in the context of
Theorem

C
, we do not know if the condition

0=\Delta(\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{A}}\}\!\!\})

can be removed from the second statement. In any case, it is interesting
that this vanishing condition is the only obstruction to the validity of the
bimodule Calabi–Yau property in our context.

Theorem

C
is a consequence of a much more precise statement. Let

\Lambda
be a basic Frobenius algebra and
I
an invertible
\Lambda
-bimodule.
Since
\Lambda
is basic, we may choose an algebra automorphism

{\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda}
, unique up to inner
automorphisms, such that
I\cong{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}
as

\Lambda
-bimodules.
8
8

8

More precisely, the map

\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Pic}(\Lambda),\qquad[\sigma]\longmapsto[{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}],

is a group isomorphism, where

\operatorname{Out}(\Lambda)\coloneqq\operatorname{Aut}(\Lambda)/\operatorname{Inn}(\Lambda)
is the outer
automorphism group of
\Lambda
and
\operatorname{Pic}(\Lambda)
is
the Picard group of invertible
\Lambda
-bimodules
[
Bol84
, Proposition 3.8]
.

We
introduce the graded algebra

\textstyle\mathbf{\Lambda}=\Lambda(\sigma,d)\coloneqq\bigoplus_{di\in d\mathbb{Z}}{}_{\sigma^{i}}{\Lambda}_{1},\qquad x*y\coloneqq\sigma^{j}(x)\cdot y,\quad|y|=dj,

where
(x,y)\mapsto x\cdot y
denotes the product in
\Lambda
. By construction,

\mathbf{\Lambda}
is concentrated in degrees
d\mathbb{Z}
and it is easy to see that

\mathbf{\Lambda}
can be described as the
\sigma
-twisted Laurent polynomial algebra
with coefficients in
\Lambda
in a variable of degree
-d
. We are chiefly
interested in the case where there exists an isomorphism

\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}(\Lambda)\cong{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}

in the stable category of
\Lambda
-bimodules, so that
\Lambda
is

twisted
(d+2)
-periodic with respect to
\sigma
, see
[
ES08
]
for a
survey and
[
Cha+20
,
JKM22
,
KLW24
]
and the references therein for more information
on this class of algebras. The inclusion of the degree
0
component

j\colon\Lambda=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{0}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{\Lambda}
induces a restriction map

j^{*}\colon\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathbf{\Lambda},\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\Lambda,\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{\bullet}\!\left(\Lambda,\mathbf{\Lambda}^{*}\right).

We are ready to state the second main result in this article, which is a
‘Calabi–Yau’ variant of
[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
.

Theorem D

(Calabi–Yau Auslander–Iyama Correspondence)
.

Let
\mathbf{k}
be a perfect field. Fix integers
d\geq 1
and
m\in\mathbb{Z}
and set

n\coloneqq md
. There are bijective correspondences between the following:

(1)

Quasi-isomorphism classes of dg
algebras
A
with the following properties:

(a)

The
0
-th cohomology
\operatorname{H}^{0}\!\left(A\right)
is a basic
finite-dimensional algebra.

(b)

The free dg
A
-module
A\in\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)
is a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting
object.

Moreover, we require the dg algebra
A
to satisfy the following property:

(c)

The dg algebra
A
is bimodule right
n
-Calabi–Yau.

(2)

Equivalence classes of pairs

(\Lambda,I)
consisting of

(a)

a basic Frobenius algebra
\Lambda
that is twisted
(d+2)
-periodic
and

(b)

an invertible
\Lambda
-bimodule
I
such that
\Omega_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}(\Lambda)\cong I

in the stable category of
\Lambda
-bimodules.

Moreover, we require the pair
(\Lambda,I)
to satisfy the following
property:

(c)

There exists an
isomorphism of graded
\mathbf{\Lambda}
-bimodules
9
9

9

We remind the reader that the
induced graded bimodule structures on shifts and linear duals involve additional
signs.

\varphi\colon\mathbf{\Lambda}(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D{\mathbf{\Lambda}},

where
\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda
is an algebra
automorphism such that
I\cong{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}
as

\Lambda
-bimodules. Moreover, given any exact sequence of

\Lambda
-bimodules

\eta\colon\quad 0\to I\to P_{d+2}\to\cdots\to P_{3}\to P_{2}\to P_{1}\to\Lambda\to 0

with projective(-injective) middle terms, we have

0=\Delta(\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{\eta}}\}\!\!\})\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+1,-d}\!\left(\mathbf{\Lambda}\right),

where
\Delta=\Delta_{\varphi}
is the Batalin–Vilkoviski operator
(
Definition

4.76
), and
\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{\eta}}\}\!\!\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)
is
the unique class whose Gerstenhaber square vanishes, that is

0=\Sq(\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{\eta}}\}\!\!\})\in\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\mathbf{\Lambda}\right),

and such that

j^{*}\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{\eta}}\}\!\!\}=\{\!\!\{{\eta}\}\!\!\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\Lambda,\mathbf{\Lambda}\right)\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{\Lambda^{e}}^{d+2}\!\left(\Lambda,I\right).

The correspondence

\eqref{it:thm:CY-correspondence:dgAs}\to\eqref{it:thm:CY-correspondence:gLambda}

is given by
A\longmapsto(H^{0}(A),H^{-d}(A))
.

In the context of
Theorem

D
, two pairs
(\Lambda,I)
and

(\Lambda^{\prime},I^{\prime})
as in (
2
) are equivalent if
there exists an algebra isomorphism

\varphi\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda^{\prime}
such that
\varphi^{*}(I^{\prime})\cong I
. We also mention that some of the conditions in
(
2c
) can be formulated purely in terms of

\Lambda
, see
Proposition

7.6
.

Removing the bimodule Calabi–Yau properties in from

Theorem

D
, that is items
(
1c
) and
(
2c
), yields precisely the condtions
in
[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
, which is the main theorem therein. Hence the theorem
follows from
[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
as soon as we verify that the
correspondence in
Theorem

D
is well defined, and establish
that the inverse correspondence in
[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
preserves the
respective Calabi–Yau properties in
Theorem

D
. Both of these
steps are non-trivial. As we explain below, our proof
of
Theorem

D
makes use of techniques of homotopy theory, in
particular of an extension to
A_{\infty}
-bimodules
[
JM25
]
of the enhanced
obstruction theory for
A_{\infty}
-algebras developed by the second-named author
in
[
Mur20
]
and, in this way, it has some formal similarities with our
proof of
[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
.

Remark
.

There is a easier version of
Theorem

D
, which follows trivially
from
[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
, where items (
1c
) and
(
2c
) are replaced by
the following weaker conditions (recall that
n=md
):

(1c’)

The additive category
\operatorname{add}(A)\subseteq\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)
is
n
-Calabi–Yau in the
sense that there are natural isomorphisms of vector spaces

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}\!\left(Q,P[n]\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D\operatorname{Hom}_{A}\!\left(P,Q\right),\qquad P,Q\in\operatorname{add}(A).

Here,
\operatorname{add}(A)
denotes the smallest additive subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)

that contains
A
and that is closed under retracts. In other words, the

n
-fold shift is a Serre functor on the additive category
\operatorname{add}(A)
.

(2c’)

There exists an isomorphism of
\Lambda
-bimodules

\varphi\colon{}_{\sigma^{m}}{\Lambda}_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D\Lambda,

where
\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Lambda
is an
algebra automorphism such that
I\cong{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}
as

\Lambda
-bimodules.

Indeed, this follows from the equivalence of pairs

(\operatorname{add}(A),[-d])\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),-\otimes_{\Lambda}{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma})

given by the Yoneda functor

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}\!\left(A,-\right)\colon\operatorname{add}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{proj}(\Lambda),

where
\Lambda=\operatorname{H}^{0}\!\left(A\right)
(see also
[
JKM22
, Corollary 4.5.20]
). There is
a further easier version of
Theorem

D
, which also follows immediately
from
[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
, where items (
1c
) and
(
2c
) are now replaced by
the following conditions:

(1c”)

The there are natural isomorphisms of vector spaces

\operatorname{Hom}_{A}\!\left(Q,P[n]\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D\operatorname{Hom}_{A}\!\left(P,Q\right),\qquad P,Q\in\operatorname{add}(A),

that exhibit the
n
-fold shift as a graded Serre functor on the

d
-sparse graded category associated to the pair
(\operatorname{add}(A),[d])
,
see
[
Boc08
, Appendix A]
. Equivalently, the
n
-fold shift is a (graded)
Serre functor on the
(d+2)
-angulated category
(\operatorname{add}(A),[d],\pentagon)
,
where
\pentagon
is the standard
(d+2)
-angulation obtained
from
[
GKO13
, Theorem 1]
.

(2c”)

There exists an isomorphism of graded
\mathbf{\Lambda}
-bimodules

\varphi\colon\mathbf{\Lambda}(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D{\mathbf{\Lambda}},

where
\sigma\colon\Lambda\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Lambda
is an algebra
automorphism such that
I\cong{}_{1}{\Lambda}_{\sigma}
as

\Lambda
-bimodules.

Thus, the main difficulty in establishing
Theorem

D
is that of
promoting a graded bimodule isomorphism as
in (
2c
)
to a
quasi-isomorphism
of dg bimodules as
in (
1c
).

1.3.
Examples

Theorems

C
and
D
are motivated by the following
examples. For
d=1

Theorems

C
and
D
can be
regarded as statements concerning Calabi–Yau additively-finite triangulated
categories. Prominent examples of such categories are the
m
-Calabi–Yau
cluster categories associated to Dynkin quivers, see
[
Bua+06
]
for the case

m=2
and
[
Kel05
, Corollary 1]
for the case
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. It is also worth
noting that, over an algebraically closed field, the weak
10
10

10

That is, the

n
-fold shift functor is a Serre functor but not necessarily in the graded
sense.

1
-Calabi–Yau additively-finite triangulated categories have been
classified by Amiot
[
Ami07
, Theorem 9.5]
, leveraging

[
BES07
, Theorem 1.2]
.

The aforementioned examples generalise as follows. Let
d\geq 1
. A (basic)
finite-dimensional algebra
H
of global dimension at most
d
is

d
-representation-finite (
d
-hereditary)
if there exists a
d
-cluster
tilting
H
-module
M\in\operatorname{mod}(H)

[
IO11
]
. The
d
-Calabi–Yau
Amiot–Guo–Keller (=AGK) cluster category of
H
,

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(\mathbf{\Pi}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{C}_{d}(H)\coloneq\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(\mathbf{\Pi}\right)/\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{fd}}\!\left(\mathbf{\Pi}\right),\qquad\mathbf{\Pi}\coloneq\mathbf{\Pi}_{d+1}(H),

is a
d
-Calabi–Yau triangulated category and

\pi\mathbf{\Pi}\in\mathcal{C}(\mathbf{\Pi})
is a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting
object
[
Ami09
,
Guo11
,
Kel05
,
IO13
,
IY20
]
, see also
[
JKM22
, Section 6.3]
.
More generally, the
md
-Calabi–Yau AGK cluster category
\mathcal{C}_{md}(H)
,
m\geq 1
, admits a
d\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting
object
[
OT12
, Section 5]
.
11
11

11

In
[
OT12
]
the authors treat the
case
m=2
but, relying on the results in
[

,
IY20
]
, their methods
generalise to the case

[
Jas17
,
JK
]
.

1.4.
Applications

1.4.1.
Contractibility of curves in Calabi–Yau threefolds

Our main result has potential applications in algebraic geometry. Consider the
polyomial algebra
\mathbf{k}[u^{-1}]
with the variable
u^{-1}
placed in
cohomological degree
-2
. A dg algebra is said to be

\mathbf{k}[u^{-1}]
-enhanced
if it is isomorphic to a dg
\mathbf{k}[u^{-1}]
-algebra
in the homotopy category of dg
\mathbf{k}
-algebras. Hua and Keller conjecture that
the derived deformation (dg) algebra
\Gamma=\Gamma_{C}^{Y}
associated to a nc
rigid rational curve
C\subset Y
in a quasi-projective Calabi–Yau threefold is

\mathbf{k}[u^{-1}]
-enhanced if and only if the curve is
contractible
[
HK24
, Conjecture 6.8]
(we refer the reader to

loc. cit.
for an explanation of the terminology). Moreover, they prove
the necessity of their conjecture by showing that, when the curve
C
contracts,
the dg algebra
\Gamma
is quasi-isomorphic to the connective cover

\tau^{\leq 0}A
of a dg algebra
A
that satisfies the conditions in

Theorem

C
for
d=2
and
m=1
and such that the compact derived
category
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)
is quasi-equivalent to the dg category of maximal
Cohen–Macaulay modules over the ring of formal functions of the singularity
underlying the contraction, see
[
HK24
, Proposition 6.9]
and Keller’s
appendix to
[
JKM22
]
and
[
JKM24
]
, where the role of the main theorem in

[
JKM22
]
in relation to the proof of the Donovan–Wemyss
Conjecture
[
DW16
]
is also explained.
12
12

12

A proof of the conjecture that does
not rely on
[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
has appeared recently in
[
KLW24
]
.
The
key point is that the latter dg category of maximal Cohen–Macaulay modules is
linear over the algebra of Laurent polynomials
\mathbf{k}[u,u^{-1}]
as a consequence
of a famous theorem of Eisenbud
[
Eis80
]
. Therefore it is interesting to
find sufficient criteria for a dg algebra to be
\mathbf{k}[u,u^{-1}]
-enhanced.

A necessary condition for a dg algebra
A
to be
\mathbf{k}[u,u^{-1}]
-enhanced is the
existence of an isomorphism
A[2]\cong A
in the derived category of dg

A
-bimodules. Suppose given a dg algebra
A
that satisfies the assumptions in

Theorem

C
for
d=2
and
m=1
; in particular,

A[2]\cong DA

in the derived category of dg
A
-bimodules. Suppose now that there exists a
further isomorphism

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)(2)\cong\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right).

If the additional condtions in
Theorem

C
are satisfied when
applied to the pair
(\operatorname{H}^{0}\!\left(A\right),\operatorname{H}^{-2}\!\left(A\right))
for
d=2
and
m=0
, the theorem
yields the existence of an isomorphism
A\cong DA
in the derived category of dg

A
-modules and therefore

A[2]\cong DA\cong A;

in particular the perfect derived category
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)
is
2
-periodic.
It would therefore be interesting to determine if such a dg algebra
A
is
necessarily
\mathbf{k}[u,u^{-1}]
-enhanced.

1.4.2.
Self-injective Jacobian algebras

Another potential application is towards a representation-theoretic
characterisation of the self-injective Jacobian algebras. Suppose that
\mathbf{k}

is an algebraically closed field of characteristic
0
. Let
Q
be a finite
quiver, and recall that a potential
W
on
Q
is the datum of a (possibly
infinite) linear combination of cycles of length at least
2
in
Q
. To the
pair
(Q,W)
, Derksen, Weyman and Zelevinsky
[
DWZ08
]
associate the Jacobian algebra

J(Q,W)
. When
J(Q,W)
is finite-dimensional, then it is also

1
-Iwanaga–Gorenstein, for
J(Q,W)
can be realised as the endomorphism
algebra of a
2
-cluster tilting object in a
2
-Calabi–Yau triangulated
category
[
Ami09
,
KR07
]
. Moreover,
J(Q,W)
is in self-injective
(=
0
-Iwanaga–Gorenstein) if and only if it is the endomorphism algebra of a

2\mathbb{Z}
-cluster tilting object
[
HI11
]
. Consequently, self-injective Jacobian
algebras are twisted
4
-periodic with respect to the Nakayama automorphism of
the algebra, which moreover satisfies the conditions in

Proposition

7.6
with
d=2
and
m=1
. In view of work
by Keller and Liu
[
KL23
,
KL23
]
and
Theorem

D
, it is
tempting to conjecture that these properties almost characterise this class of
algebras, see also
[
JKM22
, Section 6.3]
and the discussion below.

1.5.
Future work

Bimodule Calabi–Yau properties for dg algebras admit refinements to what are
called Calabi–Yau
structures

[
KS06
]
, see
[
BD19
,
KW21
]
for
interesting applications of these refined notions in representation theory of
finite-dimensional algebras and elsewhere in mathematics. In future work we hope
to investigate whether the dg algebras
A
in

Theorem

D
(
1
) admit a

right Calabi–Yau structure
, that is a class
\widetilde{\varphi}\in D\mathrm{HC}_{-n}(A)
in the linear dual of the cyclic homology of
A
whose
image

\varphi\in D\mathrm{HH}_{-n}(A)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{}}\!\left(A^{e}\right)}\!\left(A[n],DA\right)

in the Hochschild homology of
A
induces an isomorphism

{\varphi\colon A[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}DA}

in the derived
category of dg
A
-bimodules. Besides being known to hold in numerous examples,
the existence of right Calabi–Yau structures is relevant not least due to the
recent proof by Keller and Liu
[
KL23
,
KL23a
]
of a modified version of
Amiot’s conjecture
[
Ami11
]
on the characterisation of the
2
-Calabi–Yau
cluster categories associated to quivers with potential (and of a
higher-dimensional generalisation thereof). Indeed, in addition to working in
the pseudo-compact setting, the main modification to the conjecture is an
additional assumption of existence of a right Calabi–Yau structure at the level
of enhancements—rather than the mere Calabi–Yau property at the level of
triangulated categories.

1.6.
Outline of the proof of
Theorem

D

Provided that the correspondence in
Theorem

D
is well
defined, it is injective for it is the restriction to a subset of the bijective
correspondence in
[
JKM22
, Theorem A]
. Hence, it is enough to show that the
inverse correspondence in the latter theorem restricts to a map

\eqref{it:thm:CY-correspondence:gLambda}\to\eqref{it:thm:CY-correspondence:dgAs}

under the additional assumptions in
Theorem

D
. Notice,
however, that the right Calabi–Yau property is a statement about dg

A
-bimodules rather than about the dg algebra
A
itself. For this reason, we
need to appeal to an enhanced obstruction theory for the existence and
uniqueness of
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structures that is developed in
[
JM25
]

very much along the lines of its precursor for
A_{\infty}
-algebras introduced by
the second-named author in
[
Mur20
]
. In particular, we leverage a novel
cohomology theory that we call Massey bimodule cohomology
(
Definition

6.12
) that permits us to establish the following theorem, a
Calabi–Yau analogue of
[
JKM22
, Theorem B]
, from which

Theorem

D
follows using also computations similar to those
carried out in
[
Mur22
,
JKM22
]
.

Theorem E
.

Let
A
be dg algebra whose cohomology is concentrated in degrees
d\mathbb{Z}
,

d\geq 1
. Suppose that the following Massey bimodule cohomology
satisfies
13
13

13

Here and throughout the article, we write
A(0)
to
emphasise that we wish to treat
A
as a bimodule rather than as an
algebra.

\operatorname{EM}_{\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)^{e}}^{p+1,-p}\!\left(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{A\ltimes A(0)}}\}\!\!\}\right)=0,\qquad p>d.

Suppose also that there exists an isomorphism of graded
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
-bimodules

\varphi\colon\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)

for some
n\in d\mathbb{Z}
. If we have

0=\Delta(\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{A}}\}\!\!\})\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+1,-d}\!\left(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\right)=\operatorname{Ext}_{A^{e}}^{d+1,-d}\!\left(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\right),

then there exists a quasi-isomorphism of dg
A
-bimodules
A[n]\simeq DA
,
which is to say that
A
is bimodule right
n
-Calabi–Yau.

The class
\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{A\ltimes A(0)}}\}\!\!\}
, that we call the
bimodule
Universal Massey product of length
d+2
, can be defined for any pair
(A,M)

consisting of a dg algebra and a dg bimodule over it whose cohomologies are
concentrated in degrees
d\mathbb{Z}
. This new obstruction class lives in a cohomology
theory that we call the bimodule Hochschild cohomology and which is of independent
interest.
Theorem

E
can be regarded as a Kadeishvili-type
theorem in the sense that it guarantees, given the vanishing of certain
obstructions of Hochschild type, the existence a quasi-isomorphism
A[n]\simeq DA
as soon as its holds at the cohomological level. For the sake of comparison,
recall that Kadeishvili’s Formality Theorem
[
Kad88
]
says that, given the
vanishing of certain obstructions of Hochschild type, two dg algebras whose
cohomologies are isomorphic as graded algebras are already quasi-isomorphic as
dg algebras.

In general, given a
graded
algebra
A
and a
graded

A
-bimodule

M
, the bimodule Hochschild cochain complex
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,M\right)
is part of a
standard triangle

\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(M,M\right)\![-1]\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,M\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(M,M\right)

of differential bigraded vector spaces. Here,
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
is the familiar
Hochschild complex of
A
and

\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(M,M\right)\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}\!\left(\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}\otimes_{A}M\otimes_{A}\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet},M\right)

is the cochain complex that computes the bigraded vector space
\operatorname{Ext}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(M,M\right)
of
graded-bimodule self-extensions, and which is defined in terms of the bar
resolution
\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}
of
A
. The connecting map in the above triangle

\displaystyle\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(M,M\right)

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\longmapsto{\mathrm{id}_{M}}\cdot{c}-{c}\cdot{\mathrm{id}_{M}}

is given by the commutator with the identity map of
M
of the natural left and
right actions of
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
on
\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(M,M\right)
that correspond to the derived tensor
product over
A
of morphisms in the derived category of
A
-bimodules. The
upshot is the existence of long exact sequences of graded vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}_{A^{e}}^{n-1,*}\!\left(M,M\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{n,*}\!\left(A\,|\,M\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{n,*}\!\left(A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Ext}_{A^{e}}^{n,*}\!\left(M,M\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{n+1,*}\!\left(A\,|\,M\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{HH}^{n+1,*}\!\left(A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

in which the connecting maps vanish precisely when the graded
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
-bimodule

\operatorname{Ext}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(M,M\right)
is graded-symmetric, see
Proposition

4.42
. For example, this
vanishing occurs when
M=A
is the diagonal bimodule since
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
is
graded-commutative with respect to the cup product. In fact, in the latter case
there is an explicit isomorphism of Gerstenhaber algebras (with respect to the
total degree)

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right){[\varepsilon]}/(\varepsilon^{2}),

where
\varepsilon
is a central element of bidegree
(1,0)
, see

Corollary

4.74
. Ultimately, this isomorphism is what permits us to relate the
obstruction to the validity of the bimodule right Calabi–Yau property in

Theorem

E
, and then also in

Theorems

C
and
D
, to the BV operator, see

Proposition

6.20
.

Returning to the case of dg algebras and dg bimodules with
d
-sparse
cohomology, the bimodule universal Massey product

\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{A\ltimes M}}\}\!\!\}\in\operatorname{HH}^{d+2,-d}\!\left(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\,|\,\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right)\right)

is defined in terms of any minimal model of the pair
(A,M)
that is, by
definition, it is given by a minimal model of
A
as an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and a
compatible minimal model of
M
as an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule. For this reason, in
order to prove
Theorem

E
, we first prove a variant for minimal

A_{\infty}
-algebras (
Theorem

6.21
), for which we are required to
study
A_{\infty}
-bimodules in some detail as well.

1.7.
Structure of the article

The proofs of

Theorems

C
,
D
and
E
require
extensive preparations of general nature. We hope that we have made the article
more accessible by not skimping on these preliminaries. In

Section

2
we recall basic aspects of the theory of dg algebras
that are needed throughout the article. In
Section

3
we
recall the definiton and fundamental algebraic structure on the Hochschild
cohomology of a graded algebra. In
Section

4
we recall
from
[
JM25
]
the definition and algebraic structure of the bimodule
Hochschild cohomology associated to a graded bimodule, and study the bimodule
Hochschild cohomology of the diagonal bimodule in some detail therein, as this
is our main object of interest. In
Section

5
, we recall various aspects
of the theory of
A_{\infty}
-algebras and
A_{\infty}
-bimodules that are needed to
prove the crucial
Theorem

E
and its
A_{\infty}
-variant

Theorem

6.21
. In
Section

6
, we recall from

[
Mur20
,
JKM22
,
JM25
]
the definition of the Hochschild–Massey cochain
complex and its variant for bimodules as well as important results concerning
them, and use these prove
Theorem

6.21
and

Theorem

E
. Finally, in
Section

7
, we give our proofs of

Theorems

C
and
D
using

Theorem

E
—it is only in this final section that the specific
context of
Theorems

C
and
D
becomes relevant.

.

We work over an arbitrary field
\mathbf{k}
. We say that an object
c
in a

\mathbf{k}
-linear category in which the Krull–Remak–Schmidt Theorem holds (for
example, in an additive category with finite-dimensional morphism spaces and
split idempotents
[
Kra15
, Cor. 4.4]
) is
basic
if in any
decomposition
c=c_{1}\oplus c_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus c_{n}
into indecomposable
objects, there is an isomorphism
c_{i}\cong c_{j}
if and only if
i=j
. A
finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
is
basic
if the regular
representation of
\Lambda
is basic as an object of its category of
finite-dimensional (projective) modules. The Jacobson radical of a
finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
is denoted by
J_{\Lambda}
; our main
results require the ground field to be perfect or, more generally, that the
semisimple algebra
\Lambda/J_{\Lambda}
is separable over
\mathbf{k}
. Finally, we
compose morphisms in a category as functions: the composite of morphisms

f\colon x\to y
and
g\colon y\to z
is the morphism
gf=g\circ f\colon x\to z
.

\operatorname{HM}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{A}}\}\!\!\}\right)

Hochschild–Massey cohomology of
A

(
6.8
)

\operatorname{CM}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(M,\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{A\ltimes{M}}}\}\!\!\}\right)

Massey bimodule cochain complex of
M

(
6.13
)

d_{\mathrm{BimHM}}

Massey bimodule differential

(
6.14
)

\operatorname{EM}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(M,\{\!\!\{{m_{d+2}^{A\ltimes{M}}}\}\!\!\}\right)

Massey bimodule cohomology of

(

)

The various Hochschild-type cochain complexes considered in this
article, with their cohomologies and operations. Here,
A
is a graded
algebra and
M
is a graded
A
-bimodule. In the two bottom blocks,
A
and

M
are assumed to be concentrated in degrees
d\mathbb{Z}
,
d\geq 1
, and are
endowed with minimal
A_{\infty}
-structures.

2.
Differential graded algebras

In order to fix notations and sign conventions, in this preliminary section we
recall the basic aspects of the representation theory of differential graded
algebras. All the material in this section is standard and therefore our
exposition is rather brief; our main references are
[
Kel94
,
Kel06
]
.

2.1.
Differential graded vector spaces

2.1
.

A
differential graded (=dg) vector space
is a pair
V=(V^{\sharp},d_{V})
,
consisting of a graded vector space
V^{\sharp}=\coprod_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V^{i}
and a
linear map
{d=d_{V}\colon V^{\sharp}\to V^{\sharp}}
such that
d(V^{i})\subseteq V^{i+1}
and
d\circ d=0
. A
morphism of dg vector spaces

f\colon V\to W
is a linear map such that
d_{W}\circ f=f\circ d_{W}
. The collection of dg
vector spaces and their morphism form an abelian category in the apparent way.

2.2
.

Let
V
be a dg vector space. For a homogeneous element
v\in V^{i}
we write

|v|\coloneqq i
. Most formulas involving elements of a dg vector space are
given in terms of their homogeneous elements, even if this assumption is not
made explicit in the text. This abuse of language should not lead to
confusion.

2.3
.

There is an apparent equivalence between the category of cochain complexes of
vector spaces and that of dg vector spaces that associates to a dg vector
space
V
the cochain complex
(V^{i},d|_{V^{i}})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
; in particular, a
morphism of dg vector spaces
f\colon V\to W
is uniquely determined by its
restrictions

f^{i}\colon V^{i}\longrightarrow W^{i},\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z}.

In the sequel we treat this equivalence of categories as an identification and
pass between the two notions as is convenient from the context.

The category of dg vector spaces has a symmetric monoidal structure that we now
recall.

2.4
.

The tensor product of two dg vector spaces
V
and
W
is the dg vector space

V\otimes W
with homogeneous components

(V\otimes W)^{k}\coloneqq\coprod_{i+j=k}V^{i}\otimes W^{j},\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z},

and the differential

d_{V\otimes W}(v\otimes w)\coloneqq d_{v}(v)\otimes w+(-1)^{|v|}v\otimes d_{W}(w).

The braiding—responsible for the Koszul sign rule—is given by

V\otimes W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}W\otimes V,\qquad v\otimes w\mapsto(-1)^{|v||w|}w\otimes v.

The tensor product of two morphisms of dg vector spaces

{f\colon V_{1}\longrightarrow W_{1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{g\colon V_{2}\longrightarrow W_{2}}

is the morphism

(f\otimes g)(v\otimes w)\coloneqq f(v)\otimes g(w),\qquad v\in V_{1},\ w\in W_{1}.

This symmetric monoidal structure is closed, with the following internal

\operatorname{Hom}
.

2.5
.

Let
V
and
W
be dg vector spaces. We defined
\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V,W\right)
to be the
dg vector space with the homogeneous components

\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}^{i}\!\left(V,W\right)\coloneqq\prod_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V^{i},W^{i+k}\right),\qquad k\in\mathbb{Z},

and the differential

(2.6)

\partial_{V,W}(f)\coloneqq d_{W}\circ f-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ d_{V},\qquad f\in\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V,W\right).

A morphism
f\in\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}^{i}\!\left(V,W\right)
is said to be
homogeneous
of
degree
|f|\coloneqq i
. The
evaluation map

(2.7)

\operatorname{ev}=\operatorname{ev}_{V,W}\colon\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V,W\right)\otimes V\longrightarrow W,\qquad f\otimes v\longmapsto f(v),

is a moprhism of dg vector spaces and is the canonical choice of counit for an
adjunction
(-\otimes V)\dashv\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V,-\right)
.

2.8
.

Suppose given morphisms of vector spaces

{f\colon V_{1}\longrightarrow W_{1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{g\colon V_{2}\longrightarrow W_{2}}

that at are homogeneous but not necessarily of degree
0
. The Koszul sign
rule implies that there additional signs arise when evaluating the map

f\otimes g
in homogeneous elementary tensors:

(f\otimes g)(v\otimes w)\coloneqq(-1)^{|g||v|}f(v)\otimes g(w),\qquad v\in V_{1},\ w\in W_{1}.

2.9
.

Graded vector spaces, viewed as dg vector spaces with vanishing differential,
form a full subcategory of that of dg vector spaces, and the above closed
symmetric monoidal structure restricts to this full subcategory.

2.10
.

Let
V
be a dg vector space. The
(
\mathbf{k}
-)linear dual
of
V
is the dg
vector space

DV\coloneqq\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V,\mathbf{k}\right).

It has the homogeneous components (notice the minus sign)

(DV)^{i}=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V^{-i},\mathbf{k}\right),\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z},

and the differential (notice the minus sign)

\partial(f)=\partial_{V,\mathbf{k}}(f)=-(-1)^{|f|}f\circ d_{V},\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z}.

The additonal minus sign in the differential, which stems from

eq.

2.6
, is necessary when considering bimodule structures on
V

and its linear dual (see also
Example

2.11
).

2.11
.

Let
V
and
W
be dg vector spaces. Then, the map

\displaystyle D\colon\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V,W\right)\longrightarrow\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(DW,DV\right)

\displaystyle f\longmapsto(f^{*}\colon g\longmapsto(-1)^{|f||g|}gf),

is a morphism of dg vector spaces.

2.12
.

Let
V
be a dg vector space. Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we let
V[n]
be the dg vector
space with the homogeneous components

V[n]\coloneqq V^{i+n},\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z},

and the differential
d_{V[n]}\coloneqq(-1)^{n}d_{V}
. Given a further dg vector
space and
{f\in\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}^{i}\!\left(V,V\right)}
, we define the morphism

f[n]\in\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}^{i}\!\left(V[n],W[n]\right)
by the formula

f[n](v)\coloneqq(-1)^{ni}f(v),\qquad v\in V.

2.13
.

We make use the following standard notational device
14
14

14

Rigorously, we
let
\mathbf{s}^{n}\coloneqq 1_{\mathbf{k}}\in\mathbf{k}[n]
and identify
V[n]
with the tensor
product
\mathbf{k}[n]\otimes V
.
that makes it easier to keep track of degrees
and Koszul signs when shifts of dg vector spaces are involved: For a
homogeneous element
v\in V^{i}
, we write
\mathbf{s}^{n}v\in V[n]
for the
corresponding homogeneous element of degree
|\mathbf{s}^{n}v|=i-n
. In particular, we
computing Koszul signs we may regard
\mathbf{s}^{n}
as a formal symbol of degree

-n
. With this convention in mind, the differential of
V[n]
is given by

d_{V[n]}(\mathbf{s}^{n}v)=(-1)^{n}\mathbf{s}^{n}d_{V}(v),\qquad v\in V.

Similarly, for
f\in\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}^{i}\!\left(V,V\right)
, the morphism

f[n]\in\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}^{i}\!\left(V[n],W[n]\right)
is given by the formula

f[n](\mathbf{s}^{n}v)=(-1)^{n|f|}\mathbf{s}^{n}f(v)=(-1)^{ni}\mathbf{s}^{n}f(v),\qquad v\in V.

2.14
.

Let
U
and
V
and
W
be dg vector spaces and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then, the map

\displaystyle\varphi\colon U\otimes V[n]\otimes W

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(U\otimes V\otimes W)[n]

\displaystyle u\otimes\mathbf{s}^{n}v\otimes w,

\displaystyle\longmapsto(-1)^{n|u|}\mathbf{s}^{n}(u\otimes v\otimes w),

is an isomorphism of dg vector spaces. Moreover, for homogeneous morphisms of
dg vector spaces (not necessarily of degree
0
)

f\colon U_{1}\longrightarrow U_{2},\qquad g\colon V_{1}\longrightarrow V_{2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad h\colon W_{1}\longrightarrow W_{2},

the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{1}\otimes V_{1}[n]\otimes W_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{1}\otimes V_{1}\otimes W_{1})[n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{2}\otimes V_{2}[n]\otimes W_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(U_{2}\otimes V_{2}\otimes W_{2})[n].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\otimes g[n]\otimes h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f\otimes g\otimes h)[n]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

2.15
.

Let
V
and
W
be dg vector spaces and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. A morphism

{f\in\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V,W\right)}
can be regarded as an element

{f\mathbf{s}^{n}\in\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V[-n],W\right)}
of degree
|f|-n
via the formula

f\mathbf{s}^{n}(\mathbf{s}^{-n}v)\coloneqq f(v),\qquad v\in V.

Moreover, the morphism

\varphi\colon\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V,W\right){[n]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V[-n],W\right),\qquad\mathbf{s}^{n}f\longmapsto(-1)^{n|f|}f\mathbf{s}^{n},

is an isomorphism of dg vector spaces.

2.16
.

Let
V
be a dg vector space. Recall that the
cohomology
of
V
is the
graded vector space

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(V\right)\coloneqq\ker d_{V}/\img d_{V}.

A morphism between dg vector spaces
f\colon V\to W
is a

quasi-isomorphism
if the induced map

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(f\right)\colon\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(V\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(W\right),\qquad[v]\longmapsto[f(v)],

is an isomorphism of graded vector spaces.

2.17
.

Since we work over a field, a morphism between dg vector spaces
f\colon V\to W
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if it is a
homotopy equivalence
,
that is if there exists a morphism of dg vector spaces
g\colon W\to V
such
that

\displaystyle[g\circ f]=[\mathrm{id}_{V}]

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{H}^{0}\!\left(\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(V,V\right)\right)

and

\displaystyle[f\circ g]=[\mathrm{id}_{W}]

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{H}^{0}\!\left(\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(W,W\right)\right).

2.2.
Differential graded algebras

A
differential graded (=dg) algebra
is a dg vector space
A
endowed with
an associative and unital multiplication law

A\otimes A\longrightarrow A,\qquad x\otimes y\longmapsto x\cdot y=xy,

given by a morphism of dg vector spaces. Explicitly, the multiplication law
satisfies

|x\cdot y|=|x|+|y|,\qquad x,y\in A,

as well as the
graded Leibniz rule

d(x\cdot y)=d(x)\cdot y+(-1)^{|x|}x\cdot d(y),\qquad x,y\in A.

The cohomology of a dg algebra
A
is a graded algebra (=dg algebra with
vanishing differential) with the induced multiplication law

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),\qquad[x]\otimes[y]\longmapsto[xy].

A
morphism of dg algebras

f\colon A\to B
is a morphism of dg vector
spaces such that
f(1_{A})=1_{B}
and

f(x\cdot y)=f(x)\cdot f(y),\qquad x,y\in A.

Such a morphism of dg algebras induces a morphism of graded algebras

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(f\right)\colon\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(B\right),\qquad[x]\longmapsto[f(x)].

A morphism of dg algebras
f
is a
quasi-isomorphism
if
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(f\right)
is an
isomorphism of graded algebras (equivalently, an isomorphism of graded vector
spaces).

2.3.
Differential graded modules

Let
A
be a dg algebra.

2.18
.

A
differential graded (=dg) right
A
-module
is a dg vector space
M

endowed with an action map

M\otimes A\longrightarrow A,\qquad m\otimes x\longmapsto m\cdot x,

given by a morphism of dg vector spaces and such that, for all
m\in M
and
for all
x,y\in A
, the following equalities are satisfied:

\displaystyle m\cdot 1

\displaystyle=m,

\displaystyle(m\cdot x)\cdot y

\displaystyle=m\cdot(xy).

2.19
.

There is an apparent notion of dg
left

A
-module and all of the above
discussion applies
mutatis mutandis
to these. Furthermore, left dg

A
-modules identify with right dg
A^{\mathrm{op}}
-modules, where
A^{\mathrm{op}}
is the dg
algebra with the same underlying dg vector space as
A
but with the opposite
multiplication law:

A^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes A^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow A^{\mathrm{op}},\qquad x\otimes y\longmapsto(-1)^{|x||y|}yx.

For this reason we omit the adjective ‘right’ in the sequel.

2.20
.

The multiplication of dg algebra
A
endows it with the structure of a dg

A
-module that we refer to as the
regular dg
A
-module
.

2.21
.

Let
M
be a dg
A
-module and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. The dg vector space
M[n]
is a dg

A
-module with the action map

M[n]\otimes A\longrightarrow M[n],\qquad\mathbf{s}^{n}(m)\otimes x\longmapsto\mathbf{s}^{n}(mx).

2.22
.

The cohomology of a dg
A
-module
M
has the structure of an
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
-module
with the induced action map

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right),\qquad[m]\otimes[x]\longmapsto[m\cdot x].

2.23
.

A
morphism of dg
A
-modules

f\colon M\to N
is a morphism of dg
vector spaces such that, for all
x\in A
and for all
m\in M
,

f(m\cdot x)=f(m)\cdot x.

Such a morphism of dg
A
-modules induces a morphism of graded
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
-modules

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(f\right)\colon\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(N\right),\qquad[m]\longmapsto[n].

A morphism of dg
A
-modules
f
is a
quasi-isomorphism
if
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(f\right)
is
an isomorphism of graded
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
-modules (equivalently, an isomorphism of
graded vector spaces).

2.24
.

The
derived category

\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{}}\!\left(A\right)
of dg
A
-modules, defined as the
localisation of the category of dg
A
-modules at the class of
quasi-isomorphisms, is a (locally small) compactly generated triangulated

\mathbf{k}
-category. The
perfect derived category
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{c}}\!\left(A\right)
of
A
is,
by definition, the smallest triangulated subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{}}\!\left(A\right)
that
contains
A
as closed under retracts; as the notation indicates, it coincides
with the full subcategory of
\operatorname{D}^{\mathrm{}}\!\left(A\right)
spanned by the compact
objects
[
Kel94
]
.

2.4.
Differential graded bimodules

Let
A
be a dg algebra.

2.25
.

A
differential graded (=dg)
A
-bimodule
is a dg vector space
M

endowed with an action map

A\otimes M\otimes A\longrightarrow A,\qquad x\otimes m\otimes y\longmapsto x\cdot m\cdot y,

given by a morphism of dg vector spaces and such that, for all
m\in M
and
for all
w,x,y,z\in A
, the following equalities are satisfied:

\displaystyle(1\cdot m\cdot 1)

\displaystyle=m,

\displaystyle w\cdot(x\cdot m\cdot y)\cdot z

\displaystyle=(wx)\cdot m\cdot(yz).

2.26
.

It is customary to identify dg
A
-bimodules with dg
(A\otimes A^{\mathrm{op}})
-modules as follows: Given a dg
A
-bimodule
M
, define an right
action of
A\otimes A^{\mathrm{op}}
on (the underlying dg vector space of)
M
via

m\cdot(x\otimes y)\coloneqq(-1)^{|x||m|}x\cdot m\cdot y,\qquad m\in M,x,y\in A.

On the one hand, this identification is convenient since permits to extend all
notions available for right dg modules to dg bimodules. On the other hand,
since we are also interested to work with minimal
A_{\infty}
-bimodules later
in this article, this identification is inconvenient since the tensor product
of
A_{\infty}
-algebras is an unwieldy beast, see for
example
[
Lod11
,
Mas+21
]
.

The following dg bimodules play a crucial role in this article.

2.27
.

The multiplication of dg algebra
A
endows it with the structure of a dg

A
-bimodule that we refer to as the
diagonal dg
A
-bimodule
.

2.28
.

Let
M
be a dg
A
-biomdule. The dg vector space
DM=\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(M,\mathbf{k}\right)
is
a dg
A
-bimodule with the action map

(x\cdot f\cdot y)(m)\coloneqq(-1)^{|x|(|f|+|y|+|m|)}f(y\cdot m\cdot x),\qquad x,y\in A,\ m\in M,f\in DM.

Notice that, since we work over a field, there is a canonical isomorphism of
graded
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
-bimodules
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(DM\right)\cong D\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right)
that we treat as an
identification in the sequel.

2.29
.

Let
M
be a dg
A
-bimodule and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. The dg vector space
M[n]
is a dg

A
-bimodule with the action

(2.30)

x\cdot\mathbf{s}^{n}m\cdot y\coloneqq(-1)^{n|x|}\mathbf{s}^{n}(x\cdot m\cdot y),\qquad x,y\in A,\ m\in M.

In other words, the action map

\mu_{1,1}^{M[n]}\colon A\otimes M[n]\otimes A\longrightarrow M[n]

is defined, in terms of the action map

\mu_{1,1}^{M}\colon A\otimes M\otimes A\longrightarrow M

by the commutativity of the following diagram, in which the to horizontal map
is the isomorphisms of dg vector spaces from
Example

2.14
:

(2.31)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes M[n]\otimes A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A\otimes M\otimes A)[n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M[n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M[n].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{1,1}^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{s}^{n}\mu_{1,1}^{M}}

2.32
.

Let
M
be a dg
A
-bimodule and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. As a special case of
Example

2.29
,
we see that the dg
A
-bimodule
(DM)[n]
is endowed with the action

\displaystyle(x\cdot\mathbf{s}^{n}f\cdot y)(m)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n|x|}(\mathbf{s}^{n}(x\cdot f\cdot y))(m)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|x|(n+|f|+|y|+|m|)}f(ymx),

where
f\in DA
and
x,y\in A
and
m\in M
.

2.33
.

Let
M
be a dg
A
-bimodule and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Recall from
Example

2.15
that the
map

\displaystyle\varphi\colon(DM)[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D(M[-n]),\qquad\mathbf{s}^{n}f\longmapsto(-1)^{n|f|}f\mathbf{s}^{n}

is an isomorphism of dg vector spaces, where
f\in DM
. It is straightforward
to verify that this isomorphism is in fact an isomorphism of dg
A
-bimodules.

2.34
.

The cohomology of a dg
A
-bimodule
M
has the structure of a graded

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
-bimodule with the induced action map

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),\qquad[x]\otimes[m]\otimes[y]\longmapsto[x\cdot m\cdot y].

2.35
.

A
morphism of dg
A
-bimodules

f\colon M\to N
is a morphism of dg
vector spaces such that, for all
x,y\in A
and for all
m\in M
,

f(x\cdot m\cdot y)=x\cdot f(m)\cdot y.

Such a morphism of dg
A
-bimodules induces a morphism of graded

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
-bimodules

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(f\right)\colon\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(N\right),\qquad[m]\longmapsto[f(m)].

A morphism of dg
A
-bimodules
f
is a
quasi-isomorphism
if
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(f\right)
is
an isomorphism of graded
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
-bimodules (equivalently, an isomorphism of
graded vector spaces).

2.36
.

The
derived category of dg
A
-bimodules
is defined in the obvious way,
as the localisation of the category of dg
A
-bimodules at the class of
quasi-isomorphisms.

2.37
.

Identifying dg
A
-bimodules with (right) dg
A\otimes A^{\mathrm{op}}
-modules, we see
that the derived category of dg
A
-bimodules is also a (locally small)
compactly generated triangulated
\mathbf{k}
-category. However, we do not make
explicit use of this fact in the sequel.

2.5.
Bimodule right Calabi–Yau structures

The following is the central concept of interest in this article.

2.38
[
Kon93
,
KS06
]
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra whose cohomology is degree-wise finite dimensional and

n\in\mathbb{Z}
.

(1)

A
bimodule right
n
-Calabi–Yau structure
on
A
is a an
isomorphism

\varphi\colon A[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}DA

in the derived category of dg
A
-bimodules.

(2)

We say that
A
is
right
n
-Calabi–Yau as a bimodule
if it
admits a bimodule right
n
-Calabi–Yau structure.

2.39
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra whose cohomology is degree-wise finite-dimensional. A
bimodule right
n
-Calabi–Yau structure
\varphi\colon A[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}DA
induces an isomorphism of graded
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
-bimodules

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(\varphi\right)\colon\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)[-n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(DA\right)\cong\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(DA\right),

The isomorphisms of graded vector spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(DH)[-n]\cong D(H[n])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(DH)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi[-n]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{*}}

induce an isomorphism of vector spaces

H^{i}\cong(D(DH))^{i}=D((DH)^{-i})=D(D(H^{i})),\qquad i\in\mathbb{Z}.

which shows that the assumption that
H=\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
is degree-wise
finite-dimensional in
Definition

2.38
is necessary.

2.40
.

We warn the reader that bimodule right Calabi–Yau structures are more often
considered for
proper
dg algebras, that is dg algebras whose cohomology
has finite total dimension, an assumption that is never satisfied by a
non-zero dg algebra as in
Theorems

C
and
D
.

3.
Hochschild cohomology of graded algebras

In this section we recall, paying particular attention to the signs involved,
the definition and algebraic structure present in the Hochschild
cohomology of graded algebras.

3.1.
Differential graded Lie algebras

3.1
.

A
differential graded (=dg) Lie algebra
is a dg vector space
L

endowed with a
Lie bracket

L\otimes L\longrightarrow L,\qquad x\otimes y\longmapsto[x,y],

that is given by a morphism of dg vector spaces and such that the following
axioms are satisfied
15
15

15

We remind the reader that we make no assumption
on the characteristic of the ground field.

\displaystyle[x,x]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle|x|

\displaystyle\text{ is even},

\displaystyle[x,[x,x]]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle|x|

\displaystyle\text{ is odd},

\displaystyle[x,y]

\displaystyle=-(-1)^{|x||y|}[y,x],

\displaystyle[x,[y,z]]

\displaystyle=[[x,y],z]+(-1)^{|x||y|}[y,[x,z]].

The last equality is called the
graded Jacobi identity
.

3.2
.

By definition, the Lie bracket in a dg Lie algebra
L
satisfies

|[x,y]|=|x|+|y|,\qquad x,y\in L,

as well as the graded Leibniz rule

d([x,y])=[d(x),y]+(-1)^{|x|}[x,d(y)],\qquad x,y\in L.

3.3
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra. Then, the graded commutator

A\otimes A\longrightarrow A,\qquad x\otimes y\longmapsto xy-(-1)^{|x||y|}yx,

endows (the underlying dg vector space of)
A
with the structure of a dg Lie
algebra.

3.4
.

The cohomology of a dg Lie algebra
L
has the structure of a graded Lie
algebra (=dg Lie algebra with vanishing differential) with the induced Lie
bracket

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(L\right)\otimes\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(L\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(L\right),\qquad[x]\otimes[y]\longmapsto\Big[[x,y]\Big].

3.5
.

Let
L_{1}=(L_{1},[-,-]_{1})
and
L_{2}=(L_{2},[-,-]_{2})
be dg Lie algebras. A

morphism of dg Lie algebras

f\colon L_{1}\to L_{2}
is a morphism of dg
vector spaces that preserves the Lie bracket:

f([x,y]_{1})=[f(x),f(y)]_{2}\qquad x,y\in L_{1}.

In this case, the induced a morphism of graded vector spaces

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(f\right)\colon\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(L_{1}\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(L_{2}\right)

is a morphism of graded Lie algebras. Such a morphism
f
is a

quasi-isomorphism
if
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(f\right)
is an isomorphism of graded Lie algebras
(equivalently, and isomorphism of graded vector spaces).

3.6
.

If
f\colon L_{1}\to L_{2}
is a morphism between dg Lie algebras,

We also need the following variant of the notion of a dg Lie algebra.

3.7
.

A
shifted differential graded (=dg) Lie algebra
is a dg vector space

L
equipped with a degree
-1
Lie bracket

L\otimes L\longrightarrow L,\qquad x\otimes y\longmapsto[x,y],

such that the map

L[1]\otimes L[1]\longrightarrow L[1]\qquad\mathbf{s}x\otimes\mathbf{s}y\longmapsto[\mathbf{s}x,\mathbf{s}y]\coloneqq\mathbf{s}[x,y],

which is a degree
0
morphism of dg vector spaces
16
16

16

Notice that

|[\mathbf{s}x,\mathbf{s}y]|=|\mathbf{s}^{x,y}|=(|x|+|y|-1)-1=(|x|-1)+(|y|-1)=|\mathbf{s}x|+|\mathbf{s}y|.

endows the dg vector space
L[1]
with the structure of a dg Lie
algebra. Explicitly,

•

the following variant of the graded Leibniz rule is satisfied

d([x,y])=[d(x),y]+(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}x|}[x,d(y)],\qquad x,y\in L,

where
|\mathbf{s}x|=|x|-1
is the shifted degree,

•

and the following equations are satisfied for all
x,y,z\in L
:

\displaystyle[x,x]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle|x|

\displaystyle\text{ is odd},

\displaystyle[x,[x,x]]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle|x|

\displaystyle\text{ is even},

(3.8)

\displaystyle[x,y]

\displaystyle=-(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}x||\mathbf{s}y|}[y,x],

\displaystyle[x,[y,z]]

\displaystyle=[[x,y],z]+(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}x||\mathbf{s}y|}[y,[x,z]].

3.2.
Gerstenhaber algebras

.

3.9
[
Ger63
]
.

A
Gerstenhaber algebra
is a graded vector space
G
equipped with the
following structures:

•

The graded vector space
G
is a shifted graded Lie algebra with degree

-1
Lie bracket

G\otimes G\longrightarrow G,\qquad x\otimes y\longmapsto[x,y].

•

The graded vector space
G
is endowed with a graded-commutative (not
necessarily unital) algebra structure, that is
G
is equipped with an
associative multiplication map

G\otimes G\longrightarrow G,\qquad x\otimes y\longmapsto x\cdot y,

given by a morphism of graded vector spaces and such that

x\cdot y=(-1)^{|x||y|}y\cdot x,\qquad x,y\in G.

These structures must be compatible in the sense that
Gerstenhaber
relation
must be satisfied:

(3.10)

[x,y\cdot z]=[x,y]\cdot z+(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}x||y|}y\cdot[x,z],\qquad x,y,z\in G,

where
|\mathbf{s}x|=|x|-1
is the shifted degree. If
\chark{\mathbf{k}}=2
, a Gerstenhaber
algebra must be equipped with an additional map called
Gerstenhaber
square

\Sq\colon G\longrightarrow G,\qquad x\longmapsto\Sq(c),

that satisfies

|\Sq(x)|=2|x|-1,\qquad x\in G.

In general, this map is not linear nor is an abelian group homomorphism. The
Gerstenhaber square must satisfy the following equations for all
x,y\in G
:

(3.11)

\displaystyle\Sq(x+y)

\displaystyle=\Sq(x)+\Sq(y)+[x,y],

(3.12)

\displaystyle\Sq(x\cdot y)

\displaystyle=\Sq(x)\cdot y^{2}+x\cdot[x,y]\cdot y+x^{2}\cdot\Sq(y),

(3.13)

\displaystyle[\Sq(x),y]

\displaystyle=[x,[x,y]].

3.14
.

If
\chark{\mathbf{k}}\neq 2
, we define the Gerstenhaber square operation in a
Gerstenhaber algebra in even degrees as follows:

\Sq\colon G^{2n}\longrightarrow G^{4n-1},\qquad x\longmapsto\tfrac{1}{2}[x,x].

This operation satisfies the required equations in

Definition

3.9
whenever these make sense.

3.3.
Differential bigraded vector spaces

3.15
.

A
bigraded vector space
is a tuple of vector spaces

V^{\bullet,*}=(V^{p,q})_{(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}.

A bihomogeneous element
v\in V^{p,q}
has the
bidegree

(p,q)
and the

total degree

|v|\coloneqq p+q
. We also write
\lvert v\rvert_{\mathrm{h}}\coloneqq p

for the
horizontal degree
and
\lvert c\rvert_{\mathrm{v}}\coloneqq q
for the

vertical degree
, so that
|v|=\lvert v\rvert_{\mathrm{h}}+\lvert v\rvert_{\mathrm{v}}
. A
morphism

f\colon V^{\bullet,*}\to V^{\bullet,*}

of bidegree
(r,s)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}

between bigraded vector spaces is a tuple of linear maps

(V^{p,q}\to W^{p+r,q+s})_{(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}}.

Bigraded vector spaces an bihomogeneous morphisms of bidegree
(0,0)
form an
abelian category in the obvious way.

3.16
.

A
differential bigraded vector space
is a pair
(V^{\bullet,*},d_{V})

consisting of a bigraded vector space and a bidegree
(1,0)
differential

d_{V}\colon V^{\bullet,*}\to V^{\bullet,*}
, that is
d_{V}\circ d_{V}=0
and

d_{V}(V^{p,q})\subseteq V^{p+1,q},\qquad(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Thus,
(V^{\bullet,q},d_{V})
is a cochain complex of vector spaces for each

q\in\mathbb{Z}
. A
morphism of differential birgraded vector spaces
is a
bidegree
(0,0)
morphism of bigraded vector spaces
{f\colon V^{\bullet,*}\to W^{\bullet,*}}
such that
d_{W}\circ f=f\circ d_{V}
.

3.17
.

The
cohomology
of a differential bigraded vector space

(V^{\bullet,*},d_{V})
is the bigraded vector space

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(V^{\bullet,*},d_{V}\right)\coloneqq(\operatorname{H}^{p,q}\!\left(V^{\bullet,*},d_{V}\right))_{(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}},

where

\operatorname{H}^{p,q}\!\left(V^{\bullet,*},d_{V}\right)\coloneqq\operatorname{H}^{p}\!\left(V^{\bullet,q},d_{V}\right),\qquad(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

A morphism
f\colon(V^{\bullet,*},d_{V})\to(W^{\bullet,*},d_{W})
of differential
bigraded vector spaces is a
quasi-isomorphism
if the morphisms of
cochain complexes of vector spaces

f\colon(V^{\bullet,q},d_{V})\longrightarrow(W^{\bullet,q},d_{W}),\qquad q\in\mathbb{Z},

are quasi-isomorphisms; equivalently, the induced map

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(f\right)\colon\operatorname{H}^{p,q}\!\left(V^{\bullet,*},d_{V}\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{H}^{p,q}\!\left(W^{\bullet,*},d_{W}\right)

is an isomorphism of bigraded vector spaces.

3.18
.

Let
(V^{\bullet,*},d_{V})
be a differential bigraded vector space. Given

{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
, we let
(V^{\bullet,*}[n],d_{V[n]})
be the differential bigraded
vector space with
horizontally-shifted
bihomogeneous components

V[n]^{p,q}\coloneqq V^{p+n,q},\qquad(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},

and which is endowed with the bidegree
(1,0)
differential
d_{V[n]}\coloneqq(-1)^{n}d_{V}
. Notice that

\operatorname{H}^{p,q}\!\left(V^{\bullet,*}[n],d_{V[n]}\right)=\operatorname{H}^{p+n,q}\!\left(V^{\bullet,*},d_{V}\right),\qquad(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Similarly, we let
(V^{\bullet,*}(n)),d_{V(n)}
be the differential bigraded
vector space with vertically-shifted bihomogeneous components

V(n)^{p,q}\coloneqq V^{p,q+n}\qquad(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2},

and which is endowed with the differential
d_{V(n)}=d_{V}
. Notice that

\operatorname{H}^{p,q}\!\left(V^{\bullet,*}(n),d_{V(n)}\right)=\operatorname{H}^{p,q+n}\!\left(V^{\bullet,*},d_{V}\right),\qquad(p,q)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

3.19
.

We identify graded vector spaces with bigraded vector spaces concentrated in
horizontal degree
0
. Hence, to a graded vector space
V
we associate the
bigraded vector space with the bihomogeneous components

V^{0,j}\coloneqq V^{j},\qquad j\in\mathbb{Z},

and
V^{i,*}=0
for
i\neq 0
. Under this identification, the shift of graded
vector spaces corresponds to the
vertical
shift of bigraded vector
spaces. In particular, when dealing with graded and bigraded vector spaces in
tandem, we write
V\mapsto V(1)
for the (vertical-)shift operation on graded
vector spaces.

3.4.
Brace algebras

Our primary source of Gerstenhaber algebras are brace algebras with additional
structure, see
[
Kad88
,
Get93
,
GV95
]
. We recall the variant of the definition
that it is compatible with our preferred sign conventions.

3.20
[
GV95
]
.

A
brace algebra
is a bigraded graded vector space
B^{\bullet,*}
that
is endowed with a system of bidegree
(-n,0)

brace operations

\displaystyle B^{p_{0},q_{0}}\otimes B^{p_{1},q_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes B^{p_{n},q_{n}}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow B^{p_{0}+p_{1}+\cdots+p_{n}-n,q_{0}+q_{1}+\cdots+q_{n}}

\displaystyle n\geq 1

\displaystyle x_{0}\otimes x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{n}

\displaystyle\longmapsto{x_{0}}\left\{x_{1}\dots,x_{n}\right\},

which vanish when
p_{0}<n
, and which satisfy the
brace relation

(3.21)

{{x}\left\{y_{1},\dots,y_{p}\right\}}\left\{z_{1},\dots,z_{q}\right\}=\\
\sum(-1)^{\maltese}{x}\left\{z_{1},\dots,z_{i_{1}},{y_{1}}\left\{z_{i_{1}+1},\dots,z_{j_{1}}\right\},\dots,{y_{p}}\left\{z_{i_{p}+1},\dots,z_{j_{p}}\right\},z_{j_{p}+1},\dots,z_{q}\right\},

where the sum ranges over all interlaced tuples
0\leq i_{1}\leq j_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{p}\leq j_{p}\leq p_{0}
and the sign is determined by the
Koszul sign rule with respect to the shifted total degree:

\textstyle\maltese\coloneqq\sum_{s=1}^{p}\sum_{t=1}^{i_{s}}|\mathbf{s}y_{s}||\mathbf{s}z_{t}|=\sum_{s=1}^{p}\sum_{t=1}^{i_{s}}(|y_{s}|-1)(|z_{t}|-1).

3.22
.

The prototypical examples of brace algebras are obtained from graded
(non-symmetric) operads by combining their infinitesimal composition
operations in a natural way, see
[
GV95
, Section 1.1]
for details.
Although all the brace algebras that interest us are of this form, in this
article we try to keep the use of operadic language to a minimum as it is not
strictly necessary for our purposes.

The following result summarises the algebraic structures that we wish to extract
from a brace algebra, compare with
[
GV95
, Sections 1.1 and 1.2]
.

Definition-Proposition 3.23
.

Let
B
be a brace algebra.

(1)

The bidegree
(-1,0)

pre-Lie
product
is the binary operation

\displaystyle B^{p,q}\otimes B^{s,t}\longrightarrow B^{p+s-1,q+t}\qquad x\otimes y\longmapsto{x}\bullet{y}\coloneqq{x}\left\{y\right\}.

If
\chark{\mathbf{k}}=2
or
p+q
is even, we also consider the
Gerstenhaber
square
operation

\displaystyle\Sq\colon B^{p,q}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow B^{2p-1,2q},\qquad x\longmapsto\Sq(x)\coloneqq{x}\bullet{x}.

(2)

The graded commutator of the
pre-Lie product, called the
Gerstenhaber Lie bracket
,

\displaystyle B^{p,q}\otimes B^{s,t}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow B^{p+s-1,q+t}

\displaystyle x\otimes y

\displaystyle\longmapsto[x,y]\coloneqq{x}\bullet{y}-(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}x||\mathbf{s}y|}{y}\bullet{x},

endows (the underlying graded vector space of)
B
with the structure of a
shifted graded Lie algebra with respect to the total degree. Of course, the
Gerstenhaber Lie bracket has bidegree
(-1,0)
.

(3)

A
multiplication
on

B
is an element
m\in B^{2,0}
such that

{m}\bullet{m}={m}\left\{m\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0.

In the presence of a multiplication we consider the following additional
structure on
B
:

•

The bidegree
(0,0)

cup product
is the binary operation

\displaystyle B^{p,q}\otimes B^{s,t}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow B^{s,t}

\displaystyle x\otimes y

\displaystyle\longmapsto{x}\cdot{y}\coloneqq(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}x|}{m}\left\{x,y\right\}.

Endowed with the cup product,
B
is a graded (non-unital) algebra with
respect to the total degree.

•

The bidegree
(1,0)
map

\displaystyle d\colon B^{p,q}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow B^{p+1,q}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\longmapsto d(x)\coloneqq[m,x]={m}\bullet{c}-(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}c|}{c}\bullet{m},

is a differential:
d\circ d=0
. Consequently,
(B^{\bullet,*},d)
is a
differential bigraded vector space.

•

With the above differential,
(B^{\bullet,*},d)
is a shifted dg Lie
algebra with the Gerstenhaber Lie bracket and a (non-unital) dg algebra
with the cup product, in both cases with respect to the total degree.

•

With the induced operations, the cohomology of
(B^{\bullet,*},d)
,
which itself is naturally bigraded, is a Gerstenhaber algebra with respect
to the total degree. In particular, the cup product is graded-commutative
with respect to the total degree at the cohomological level (but in
general not at the cochain level):

{[x]}\cdot{[y]}=(-1)^{|x||y|}{[y]}\cdot{[x]},\qquad x,y\in B^{\bullet,*}.

3.24
.

A multiplication on a brace algebra is equivalent to the data of a homotopy
Gerstenhaber algebra in the sense of
[
GV95
, Definition 2]
, see Theorem 3
in
op. cit.

3.25
.

The Gerstenhaber algebra structure on the cohomology of a brace algebra
B

depends only on the binary brace defining the pre-Lie product and on braces of
the form
{m}\left\{x,y\right\}
, where
m\in B^{2,0}
is a multiplication. In what
concerns us in this article, other braces of higher arity and the brace
relation are only needed to prove the claims in

3.23
. For this reason we do not define the full
brace algebra structure on the Hochschild(-type) cochain complexes recalled in
later sections.

3.26
.

For illustration purposes, let us show that the cup product in a brace algebra

B^{\bullet,*}
with multiplication
m\in B^{2,0}
is associative: Firstly,
since
{m}\left\{m\right\}=0
and
|\mathbf{s}m|=2-1=1
, the brace relation yields

0={{m}\left\{m\right\}}\left\{x,y,z\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:brace_relation}}}{{=}}{m}\left\{{m}\left\{x,y\right\},z\right\}+(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}x|}{m}\left\{x,{m}\left\{y,z\right\}\right\}.

Therefore, since
|x\cdot y|=|x|+|y|
,

\displaystyle{({x}\cdot{y})}\cdot{z}-{x}\cdot{({y}\cdot{z})}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}x|+|\mathbf{s}({x}\cdot{y})|}{m}\left\{{m}\left\{x,y\right\},z\right\}-(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}x|+|\mathbf{s}y|}{m}\left\{x,{m}\left\{y,z\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|y|}\Big({m}\left\{{m}\left\{x,y\right\},z\right\}+(-1)^{|\mathbf{s}x|}{m}\left\{x,{m}\left\{y,z\right\}\right\}\Big)

\displaystyle=0.

Thus
{({x}\cdot{y})}\cdot{z}={x}\cdot{({y}\cdot{z})}
, as required.

3.5.
Interlude: Infinitesimal compositions

In order to simplify some formulas that arise when dealing with Hochschild-type
cochain complexes, it is convenient to introduce a minimal amount of operadic
notation. Consider homogeneous operations

\displaystyle c_{1}\colon X_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{p}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow X,

\displaystyle\lvert c_{1}\rvert_{\mathrm{h}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq p,

\displaystyle\lvert c_{1}\rvert_{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle=q,

\displaystyle|c_{1}|

\displaystyle\coloneqq p+q

\displaystyle c_{2}\colon Y_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes Y_{s}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow Y,

\displaystyle\lvert c_{2}\rvert_{\mathrm{h}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq s,

\displaystyle\lvert c_{2}\rvert_{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle=t,

\displaystyle|c_{2}|

\displaystyle\coloneqq s+t,

where
X_{1},\dots,X_{p},X
and
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{s},Y
are graded vector spaces, and the
vertical degrees are the degrees of the maps as morphisms of graded vector
spaces.

3.27
.

For each
1\leq i\leq p
such that
X_{i}=Y
, we define the
(suspended) infinitesimal
composition
