as bigraded vector spaces. We endow
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
with the unique bidegree
(1,0)

differential
d
that extends the Hochschild differential on
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
and
satisfies
d(\varepsilon)=0
and

d({c}\cdot{\varepsilon})={d_{\mathrm{Hoch}}(c)}\cdot{\varepsilon},\qquad c\in\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right).

We also extend the dg algebra structure on
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
, given by the cup product
defined by
eq.

3.53
, to
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]
by stipulating
that
\varepsilon^{2}=0
and that
\varepsilon
central with respect to the
total degree:

{\varepsilon}\cdot{c}=(-1)^{|c|}{c}\cdot{\varepsilon},\qquad c\in\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right).

In this way,
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]
becomes a dg algebra with respect to the
total degree. Moreover, by construction, the cohomology of

\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,\varepsilon\right)
is the graded algebra

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]\right)=\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^{2}),\qquad|\varepsilon|=1

of dual numbers with coefficients in
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
, where
\varepsilon
is a
central element.

We define a morphism of bigraded vector spaces

\kappa\colon\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]

as follows: For
c\in\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
we set
\kappa(c)\coloneqq c
and, for

c\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{p,q,r}\!\left(A,A\right)
with
n=p+1+q
we set

\kappa(c)\coloneqq{(c\bullet_{p+1}1_{A})}\cdot{\varepsilon}\in\operatorname{C}^{n-1,*}\!\left(A\right)\cdot\varepsilon,

where
1_{A}\in\operatorname{C}^{0,0}\!\left(A\right)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(\mathbf{k},A\right)
is the unit of the graded algebra

A
. Explicitly,

(4.69)

\kappa(c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{p+q})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:infinitesimal_composition}}}{{=}}(-1)^{q}c_{i}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},1_{A},x_{p+1},\dots,x_{p+q}).

4.70
.

For the identity morphism
\mathrm{id}_{A}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{0,0,0}\!\left(A,A\right)
,

\kappa(\mathrm{id}_{A})={1_{A}}\cdot{\varepsilon}.

4.71
.

The following description of the morphism
\kappa
is also useful: For a
cochain

c=(c_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{n-1,*}\!\left(A,A\right)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{i-1,n-i,*}\!\left(A,A\right)

we have

(4.72)

\kappa(c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{n-i}c_{i}(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},1_{A},x_{i},\dots,x_{n-1}).

4.73
.

The morphism of bigraded vector spaces

\kappa\colon\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]

is a quasi-isomorphism of dg algebras with respect to the total degree, so
that it induces an isomorphism of graded algebras

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\kappa\right)\colon\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon].

Proof.

We first prove that
\kappa
is an morphism of bigraded vector spaces. Indeed,
for a cochain
c\in\operatorname{C}^{n,r}\!\left(A\right)
,

\displaystyle d_{A|A}(c)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Hd-RelBimHC-sum}}}{{=}}[m_{2}^{A},c]+[m_{1,0}^{A},c]+[m_{0,1}^{A},c]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Hd-RelBimHC-sum-HC}}}{{=}}\underbrace{[m_{2}^{A},c]}_{\in\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)}+\underbrace{m_{1,0}^{A}\bullet_{1}c}_{\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{n,0,*}\!\left(A,A\right)}+\underbrace{m_{0,1}^{A}\bullet_{2}c}_{\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{0,n,*}\!\left(A,A\right)}

and hence

\displaystyle\kappa(d_{A|A}(c))

\displaystyle=[m_{2},c]+\underbrace{\kappa(m_{1,0}^{A}\bullet_{1}c)+\kappa(m_{0,1}^{A}\bullet_{2}c)}_{=0?}=d_{\mathrm{Hoch}}(\kappa(c)),

provided that
\kappa(m_{1,0}^{A}\bullet_{1}c)+\kappa(m_{0,1}^{A}\bullet_{2}c)=0
, which
is the case since

\displaystyle\kappa(m_{1,0}^{A}\bullet_{1}c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:kappa}}}{{=}}(m_{1,0}^{A}\bullet_{1}c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},1_{A})

and

\displaystyle\kappa(m_{0,1}^{A}\bullet_{2}c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:kappa}}}{{=}}-(m_{0,1}^{A}\bullet_{2}c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})1_{A}.

We now consider the case
c\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{p,q,r}\!\left(A,A\right)
with
p+1+q=n
. In this
case

\displaystyle d_{A|A}(c)=\underbrace{d_{\mathrm{h}}(c)}_{\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{p+1,q,r}\!\left(A,A\right)}+\underbrace{d_{\mathrm{v}}(c)}_{\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{p,q+1,r}\!\left(A,A\right)},

see
eq.

4.36
. Using
eq.

4.69
while keeping in mind that

d_{\mathrm{h}}(c)\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{p+1,q,*}\!\left(A,A\right)
, we first compute

\displaystyle\kappa(d_{\mathrm{h}}(c))

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:dh}}}{{=}}(-1)^{q}\Big(-1)^{r|x_{1}|+r}x_{1}c(x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1},1_{A},x_{p+2},\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{i+r}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{i}x_{i+1},\dots,x_{p+1},1_{A},x_{p+2},\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle+(-1)^{p+1+r}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},x_{p+1}1_{A},x_{p+2},\dots,x_{n+1})\Big),

and, since
d_{\mathrm{v}}(c)\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{p,q+1,*}\!\left(A,A\right)
,

\displaystyle\kappa(d_{\mathrm{v}}(c))

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:dv}}}{{=}}(-1)^{q+1}\Big((-1)^{p+1+r}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},1_{A}x_{p+1},\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\sum_{j=1}^{q}(-1)^{p+1+j+r}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},1_{A},x_{p+1},\dots,x_{p+j}x_{p+j+1},\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle+(-1)^{p+q+r}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},1_{A},x_{p+1},\dots,x_{n})x_{n+1}\Big),

so that

\displaystyle\kappa(d_{A|A}(c))

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+1})=(-1)^{r|x_{1}|+r+q}x_{1}c(x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1},1_{A},x_{p+2}\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{i+r+q}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{i}x_{i+1},\dots,x_{p+1}1_{A},x_{p+2},\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\sum_{j=1}^{q}(-1)^{p+j+r+q}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{p}1_{A},x_{p+2},\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}(-1)^{p+1+r}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},1_{A},x_{p+1},x_{n})x_{n+1}.

On the other hand, since
n=p+1+q
,

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{Hoch}}(\kappa(c))

\displaystyle(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Hd-full}}}{{=}}(-1)^{r|x_{1}|+r}x_{1}\kappa(c)(x_{2},\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i+r}\kappa(c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{i}x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+(-1)^{n+1+r}\kappa(c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})x_{n+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:kappa}}}{{=}}(-1)^{r|x_{1}|+r+q}x_{1}c(x_{2},\dots,x_{p+1},1_{A},x_{p+2},\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{i+r+q}\kappa(c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{i}x_{i+1},\dots,x_{p+1},1_{A},x_{p+2},\dots,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+\sum_{j=1}^{q}(-1)^{p+j+r+q}\kappa(c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},1_{A},x_{p+1},\dots,x_{p+j}x_{p+j+1}\dots,,x_{n+1})

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+(-1)^{p+1+r}c(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},1_{A},x_{p+1},\dots,x_{n})x_{n+1}.

It follows that
\kappa(d_{A|A}(c))=d_{\mathrm{Hoch}}(\kappa(c))
, as required.

We now prove that
\kappa
is compatible with the corresponding algebra
structures. It is obvious that

\kappa({c_{1}}\cdot{c_{2}})={\kappa(c_{1})}\cdot{\kappa(c_{2})},\qquad c_{1},c_{2}\in\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)

since
\kappa
acts as the identity on
\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\subseteq\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)
.
Similarly,

\kappa(\underbrace{{c_{1}}\cdot{c_{2}}}_{=0})=0={\kappa(c_{1})}\cdot{\kappa(c_{2})},\qquad c_{1},c_{2}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,A\right);

the vanihsing of the expression on the right-hand side is clear since

\varepsilon^{2}=0
, while the vanishing of the expression on the left-hand side
follows from
eq.

4.28
. We now consider the remaining cases:

•

If
c_{1}\in\operatorname{C}^{p,q}\!\left(A\right)
and
c_{2}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{s,t,u}\!\left(A,A\right)
, then

{c_{1}}\cdot{c_{2}}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{p+s,q,u}\!\left(A,A\right)
and hence

\displaystyle{\kappa(c_{1})}\cdot{\kappa(c_{2})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:kappa}}}{{=}}{c_{1}}\cdot{({(c_{2}\bullet_{s+1}1_{A})}\cdot{\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle={({c_{1}}\cdot{(c_{2}\bullet_{s+1}1_{A})})}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:cup_product-RelBimHC-HC-BimHC}}}{{=}}{((m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{1}c_{1})\bullet_{p+1}(c_{2}\bullet_{s+1}1_{A}))}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:infinitesimal_composition-associative}}}{{=}}{((m_{1,0}^{A}\bullet_{1}c_{1})\bullet_{p+1}c_{2})\bullet_{p+s+1}1_{A})}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:cup_product-RelBimHC-HC-BimHC}}}{{=}}{(({c_{1}}\cdot{c_{2}})\bullet_{p+s+1}1_{A})}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:kappa}}}{{=}}\kappa({c_{1}}\cdot{c_{2}}),

where in the fourth equality we use that
m_{1,0}^{A}=m_{2}^{A}
as morphisms
of graded vector spaces.

•

If
c_{1}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{p,q,r}\!\left(A,A\right)
and
c_{2}\in\operatorname{C}^{s,t}\!\left(A\right)
, then

{c_{1}}\cdot{c_{2}}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{p,q+s,r+t}\!\left(A,A\right)
and hence

\displaystyle{\kappa(c_{1})}\cdot{\kappa(c_{2})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:kappa}}}{{=}}{({(c_{1}\bullet_{p+1}1_{A})}\cdot{\varepsilon})}\cdot{c_{2}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{s+t}{({(c_{1}\bullet_{p+1}1_{A})}\cdot{c_{2}})}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:cup_product-RelBimHC-BimHC-HC}}}{{=}}(-1)^{s+t+p+q-1}{((m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{1}(c_{1}\bullet_{p+1}1_{A}))\bullet_{p+q+1}c_{2})}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:infinitesimal_composition-associative}}}{{=}}(-1)^{s+t+p+q-1+(s+t-1)}{(((m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{1}c_{1})\bullet_{p+1+q+1}c_{2})\bullet_{p+1}1_{A})}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{p+q}{(((m_{0,1}^{A}\bullet_{1}c_{1})\bullet_{p+1+q+1}c_{2})\bullet_{p+1}1_{A})}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:cup_product-RelBimHC-BimHC-HC}}}{{=}}{(({c_{1}}\cdot{c_{2}})\bullet_{p+1}1_{A})}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:kappa}}}{{=}}\kappa({c_{1}}\cdot{c_{2}});

here, in the fourth equality we use that
1_{A}\in\operatorname{C}^{0,0}\!\left(A\right)
and in the
fifth equality we use that
m_{1,0}^{A}=m_{2}^{A}
as morphisms of graded
vector spaces.

In order to show that
\kappa
is a quasi-isomorphism we argue as follows.
Recall that the bar resolutin of
A
is the chain complex of graded

A
-bimodules with components

\operatorname{B}(A)_{n}\coloneqq A\otimes A^{\otimes n}\otimes A,\qquad n\geq 0

and the differential

d(x_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{n+1})\coloneqq\sum_{i=0}^{n}(-1)^{i}x_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{i}x_{i+1}\otimes\cdots x_{n+1}.

The augmentation map

\operatorname{B}(A)_{0}\longrightarrow A,\qquad x_{0}\otimes x_{1}\longmapsto x_{0}x_{1},

exhibits
\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}
as a graded projective resolution of the diagonal bimodule.
The bar resolution is also equipped with the comultiplication map

\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}\longrightarrow\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}\otimes_{A}\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}

whose components

\operatorname{B}(A)_{n}\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}\operatorname{B}(A)_{i}\otimes_{A}\operatorname{B}(A)_{n-i},

are given by

(x_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{n+1})\longmapsto\sum_{i=0}^{n}(a_{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{i}\otimes 1_{A})\otimes(1_{A}\otimes a_{i+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes a_{n+1}).

Moreover,

\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}\otimes_{A}\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}\cong\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}\otimes_{A}A\otimes_{A}\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}

is also a graded projective resolution of the diagonal bimodule, as witnessed
by the augmentation map

\operatorname{B}(A)_{0}\otimes_{A}A\otimes_{A}\operatorname{B}(A)_{0}\longrightarrow A,\qquad(x_{0}\otimes x_{1})\otimes y\otimes(z_{0}\otimes z_{1})\longmapsto(x_{0}x_{1}yz_{0}z_{1}).

Since the comultiplication map is a morphism of chain complexes of projective
graded
A
-bimodules and is compatible with the corresponding augmentation, it
is a chain homotopy equivalence. It follows that the induced map

\displaystyle\widetilde{\kappa}\colon\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}\!\left(\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}\otimes_{A}A\otimes_{A}\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet},A\right)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}\!\left(\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet},A\right),

given explicitly by

\kappa(c)(x_{0},\dots,x_{n+1})=\sum_{i=0}^{n}c(x_{0},\dots,x_{i},1_{A},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n+1})

for
c\in\operatorname{C}^{n,*}\!\left(A\right)
, is also homotopy equivalence. Under the identifications

\displaystyle\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,A\right)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}\!\left(\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet}\otimes_{A}A\otimes_{A}\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet},A\right)

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)

\displaystyle\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{A^{e}}\!\left(\operatorname{B}(A)_{\bullet},A\right)

induced by the
\otimes
-
\operatorname{Hom}
adjunction, the homotopy
equivalence
\widetilde{\kappa}
corresponds to the restriction of
\kappa[1]

to
\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,A\right)\subseteq\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)
, and hence the latter map is also a
homotopy equivalence. We finish the proof by observing that the following
diagram of morphisms of differential bigraded vector spaces commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A,A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa[1]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Indeed, the leftmost square commutes by the definition of
\kappa
since the
horizontal maps are the canonical projections. That the rightmost square
commutes is a consequence of the fact that
\kappa
is a morphism of
differential bigraded vector spaces. The induced long exact sequence in
cohomology yields the desired fact that
\kappa
is a quasi-isomorphism,
compare with
Proposition

4.42
.
∎

4.74
.

The isomorphism of graded algebras

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(\kappa\right)\colon\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^{2})

induces a shifted Lie bracket and a Gerstenhaber square operation on

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]
, and hence an isomorphic Gerstenhaber algebra structure.
Moreover, the following statements hold:

(1)

The induced shifted Lie bracket extends the Gerstenhaber bracket on the
shifted graded Lie subalgebra
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\subseteq\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]
and is
uniquely determined by the following additional requirements:

\displaystyle[\varepsilon,\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle[c,\varepsilon]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right).

From these it follows that the following equations must hold:

\displaystyle[c_{1},{c_{2}}\cdot{\varepsilon}]

\displaystyle={[c_{1},c_{2}]}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle c_{1},c_{2}

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right),

\displaystyle[{c_{1}}\cdot{\varepsilon},{c_{2}}\cdot{\varepsilon}]

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle c_{1},c_{2}

\displaystyle\in\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right).

(2)

If
\chark{\mathbf{k}}=2
, then the induced Gerstenhaber square on

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]
extends the Gerstenhaber square on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
and is
more generally given by the formula

\Sq(c_{1}+{c_{2}}\cdot{\varepsilon})=\Sq(c_{1})+{(c_{2}^{2}+[c_{1},c_{2}])}\cdot{\varepsilon},\qquad c_{1},c_{2}\in\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right).

If
\chark{\mathbf{k}}\neq 2
, then the Gerstehaber square is defined in even
degrees as in
Remark

3.14
.

Proof.

Since
\kappa
acts as the identity on the shifted graded Lie subalgebra

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\subseteq\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)
, where the bimodule Gerstenhaber bracket is
given by the usual Gerstenhaber bracket, we see that the transported shifted
Lie bracket must also extend the Gerstenhaber bracket on

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\subseteq\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\![\varepsilon]
. In order to establish the remaining
constraints on the transported shifted Lie bracket, recall from

Example

4.70
that
\kappa(\mathrm{id}_{A})={1_{A}}\cdot{\varepsilon}
. Hence,

[\varepsilon,\varepsilon]=[\kappa(\mathrm{id}_{A}),\kappa(\mathrm{id}_{A})]=\kappa([\mathrm{id}_{A},\mathrm{id}_{A}])=0,

since
[\mathrm{id}_{A},\mathrm{id}_{A}]=0
, for
\mathrm{id}_{A}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{0,0,0}\!\left(A,A\right)
has odd degree

|\mathrm{id}_{A}|=1
given by its arity. Similarly, for all
c\in\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
we have

[c,\mathrm{id}_{A}]=0
(see
Example

4.17
), and hence

[c,\varepsilon]=[c,{1_{A}}\cdot{\varepsilon}]=[\kappa(c),\kappa(\mathrm{id}_{A})]=\kappa([c,\mathrm{id}_{A}])=0,\qquad c_{1},c_{2}\in\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right).

Consequently, by the Gerstenhaber relation,

\displaystyle[c_{1},{c_{2}}\cdot{\varepsilon}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Gerstenhaber_relation}}}{{=}}{[c_{1},c_{2}]}\cdot{\varepsilon}\pm\underbrace{{c_{2}}\cdot{[c_{1},\varepsilon]}}_{=0}={[c_{1},c_{2}]}\cdot{\varepsilon}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle[{c_{1}}\cdot{\varepsilon},{c_{2}}\cdot{\varepsilon}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Gerstenhaber_relation}}}{{=}}{[{c_{1}}\cdot{\varepsilon},c_{2}]}\cdot{\varepsilon}\pm{c_{2}}\cdot{[{c_{1}}\cdot{\varepsilon},\varepsilon]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Gerstenhaber_relation}}}{{=}}\underbrace{{(\pm{[c_{1},c_{2}]}\cdot{\varepsilon})}\cdot{\varepsilon}}_{=0}\pm{c_{2}}\cdot{({\underbrace{[\varepsilon,c_{1}]}_{=0}}\cdot{\varepsilon}\pm{c_{1}}\cdot{\underbrace{[\varepsilon,\varepsilon]}_{=0}})}=0.

When
\chark{\mathbf{k}}=2
, the transported Gerstehaber square on

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\langle\varepsilon\rangle
must extend that of
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
since
\kappa

acts as the identity on
\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\subseteq\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)
. Moreover, since in

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)
we have

\Sq(\mathrm{id}_{A})={\mathrm{id}_{A}}\bullet{\mathrm{id}_{A}}=\mathrm{id}_{A}\bullet_{1}\mathrm{id}_{A}=\mathrm{id}_{A},

we must have

\Sq(\varepsilon)=\Sq(\kappa(\mathrm{id}_{A}))=\kappa(\Sq(\mathrm{id}_{A}))=\kappa(\mathrm{id}_{A})=1_{A}\cdot\varepsilon=\varepsilon.

Finally, the relations satisfied by the Gerstenhaber square in

\operatorname{HH}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,A\right)
yield, for
c\in\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
,

\Sq({c}\cdot{\varepsilon})=\Sq(c)\cdot\underbrace{\varepsilon^{2}}_{=0}+{c}\cdot{{\underbrace{[c,\varepsilon]}_{=0}}\cdot{\varepsilon}}+{c^{2}}\cdot{\Sq(\varepsilon)}={c^{2}}\cdot{\varepsilon}

and then also, for
c_{1},c_{2}\in\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)
,

\displaystyle\Sq(c_{1}+{c_{1}}\cdot{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\Sq(c_{1})+\Sq({c_{1}}\cdot{\varepsilon})+[c_{1},{c_{2}}\cdot{\varepsilon}]

\displaystyle=\Sq(c_{1})+{c_{2}^{2}}\cdot{\varepsilon}+{[c_{1},c_{2}]}\cdot{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\Sq(c_{1})+{(c_{2}^{2}+[c_{1},c_{2}])}\cdot{\varepsilon}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

4.75
.

We record the following formula for later use. Suppose that there exists an
isomorphism of graded
A
-bimodules

\varphi\colon A(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}DA

for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Given a cochain

c=(c_{i})_{i=1}^{m}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{m-1,*}\!\left(A,A\right)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{i-1,m-i,*}\!\left(A,A\right),

we have
\Upsilon(c_{i})\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{m-i,i-1,*}\!\left(A,A\right)
and hence

\kappa(\Upsilon(c))(x_{1},\dots,x_{m-1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:kappa-sum}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(-1)^{i-1}\Upsilon(c_{i})(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},1_{A},x_{i},\dots,x_{m-1})

Consequently,
\kappa(\Upsilon(c))
is uniquely determined by the requirement
that, for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in A
,

\langle\mathbf{s}^{n}\kappa(\Upsilon(c))(x_{1},\dots,x_{m-1}),x_{n}\rangle_{\varphi}\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(-1)^{i-1}\langle\mathbf{s}^{n}\Upsilon(c_{i})(x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},1_{A},x_{i},\dots,x_{m-1}),x_{m}\rangle_{\varphi}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Upsilon}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}(-1)^{i-1+(m-i+1)i+\maltese}\langle\mathbf{s}^{n}c_{i}(x_{i},\dots,x_{m-1},x_{m},x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1}),1_{A}\rangle_{\varphi}\\
=-\sum_{i=1}^{m}(-1)^{i(m-1)+\maltese}\langle\mathbf{s}^{n}c_{i}(x_{i},\dots,x_{m-1},x_{m},x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1}),1_{A}\rangle_{\varphi},

where

\maltese=\textstyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}|x_{j}|\right)\Big(\sum_{k=i}^{m}|x_{k}|\Big).

Remark

4.75
motivates the introduction of the following operator in
our context, compare with
[
Tra08a
, Definition 3]
where the case of ungraded
algebras is considered. We shall return to it in later sections.

4.76
.

Suppose that there exists an isomorphism of graded
A
-bimodules

\varphi\colon A(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}DA

for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. The
Batalin–Vilkoviski (=BV) operator
is the
bidegree
(-1,0)
operator

\Delta=\Delta_{\varphi}\colon\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\right)\longrightarrow\operatorname{C}^{\bullet-1,*}\!\left(A\right)

uniquely characterised by the property that, for a cochain
c\in\operatorname{C}^{m,r}\!\left(A\right)
,
the cochain
\Delta(c)\in\operatorname{C}^{m-1,r}\!\left(A\right)
satisfies

\langle\mathbf{s}^{n}\Delta(c)(x_{1},\dots,x_{m-1}),x_{m}\rangle_{\varphi}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}(-1)^{i(m-1)+\maltese}\langle\mathbf{s}^{n}c(x_{i},\dots,,x_{m},x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1}),1_{A}\rangle_{\varphi},

for all
x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\in A
, where

\maltese=\textstyle\left(\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}|x_{j}|\right)\Big(\sum_{k=i}^{m}|x_{k}|\Big).

5.
A_{\infty}
-algebras and
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

In this section we first review several important aspects concerning

A_{\infty}
-algebras and
A_{\infty}
-bimodules that are needed to prove

Theorem

E
and its variant for minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebras
(
Theorem

6.21
).

5.1.
Prelude: More on infinitesimal compositions

The following considerations concerning infinitesimal compositions are useful
for dealing with
A_{\infty}
-equations in convenient way.

5.1
.

Let
(V,d_{V})
and
(W,d_{W})
be dg vector spaces. Then,

\partial_{V^{\otimes n},W}(c)={d_{W}}\left\{c\right\}+(-1)^{n+r}{c}\left\{d_{V}\right\},\qquad c\in\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}^{r}\!\left(V^{\otimes n},W\right).

Indeed,

\displaystyle{d_{W}}\left\{c\right\}+{c}\left\{d_{V}\right\}

\displaystyle=d_{W}\bullet_{1}c+(-1)^{n+r}\sum_{i=1}^{n}c\bullet_{i}d_{V}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:infinitesimal_composition}}}{{=}}d_{W}\circ c+(-1)^{n+r}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{n-1}c\circ(\mathrm{id}_{V}^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes d_{V}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{V}^{\otimes(n-i)})

\displaystyle=d_{W}\circ c-(-1)^{r}c\circ\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{id}_{V}^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes d_{V}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{V}^{\otimes(n-i)}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:partial}}}{{=}}d_{W}\circ c-(-1)^{r}c\circ d_{V^{\otimes n}}.

In particular, if the total degree
n+r
of
c
is even, then

\partial_{V^{\otimes n},W}(c)={d_{W}}\left\{c\right\}+{c}\left\{d_{V}\right\}.

We introduce the following variant of the operation defined by

eq.

3.33
.

5.2
.

Suppose given countable tuples

c^{(1)}=(c^{(1)}_{1},c^{(1)}_{2},c^{(1)}_{3},\dots,c^{(1)}_{n},\dots)\qquad\text{and}\qquad c^{(2)}=(c^{(2)}_{1},c^{(2)}_{2},c^{(2)}_{4},\dots,c^{(2)}_{n},\dots),

where

c^{(1)}_{n}\colon X_{1,n}\otimes\cdots\otimes X_{n,n}\longrightarrow X_{n}

and

c^{(2)}_{n}\colon Y_{1,n}\otimes\cdots\otimes Y_{n,n}\longrightarrow Y_{n}

are homogeneous morphisms of graded vector spaces. We define

{c_{1}}\left\{c_{2}\right\}
to be the tuple with the components

(5.3)

\sum_{p+q-1=n}{c_{p}^{(1)}}\left\{c_{q}^{(2)}\right\}=\sum_{p+q-1=n}\sum_{i=1}^{p}c_{p}^{(1)}\bullet_{i}c_{q}^{(2)},\qquad n\geq 1,

where the first sum ranges over all ordered decompositions
p+q-1=n
with

p,q\geq 1
and the second sum ranges over all ordered decompositions
r+s+t=n

with
r,t\geq 0
and
s\geq 1
. Notice that

(5.4)

\sum_{p+q-1=n}\sum_{i=1}^{p}c_{p}^{(1)}\bullet_{i}c_{q}^{(2)}=\sum_{r+s+t=n}c^{(1)}_{r+1+t}\bullet_{r+1}c^{(2)}_{s},\qquad n\geq 1,

which is another convenient expression for
{c_{1}^{(1)}}\left\{c_{2}^{(2)}\right\}
.

5.2.
A_{\infty}
-algebras and
A_{\infty}
-morphisms between them

We recall the definition of an
A_{\infty}
-algebra, for which we adopt the sign
conventions in
[
LH03
]
. We also recommend the articles
[
Kel01
,
Kel02
]
,
but warn the reader that the sign conventions use therein do not agree with
those in
[
LH03
]
. Finally, we remind the reader of
Remark

3.58
and

5.2
.

5.5
.

An
A_{\infty}
-algebra
is a pair
(A,m^{A})
consisting of a graded
vector space
A
and a cochain

m^{A}=(m_{1}^{A},m_{2}^{A},m_{3}^{A},\dots,m_{n}^{A},\dots)\in\prod_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{C}^{n,2-n}\!\left(A\right),

called an
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure
, such that the

A_{\infty}
-equation is satisfied:

{m^{A}}\left\{m^{A}\right\}=0.

We say that an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
(A,m^{A})
is
minimal
if
m_{1}=0
.

5.6
.

There are various notions of unitality for
A_{\infty}
-algebras,
see
[
Sei08
, Section I.2]
for a discussion of this issue and it
resolution. The notion of unitality that is most relevant for us is that of

strict unitality
, that is the existence of a degree
0
cocycle
1

which is a unit for the binary operation and such that

m_{n}(\dots,1,\dots)=0,\qquad n\geq 3.

We shall remind the reader of this assumption whenever it becomes relevant in

Proposition

6.20
,
Theorem

6.21
and
Remark

6.22
.

We now discuss alternative presentations of the
A_{\infty}
-equation(s). Our
motivation for this is two-fold: On the one hand, we wish to compare our sign
conventions with those in the literature; on the other hand, different
presentations of these equations suggest different interpretations that are
relevant for us in the sequel.

5.7
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra. According to

eq.

5.4
, the components of the
A_{\infty}
-equation

{m^{A}}\left\{m^{A}\right\}=0
are given by

(5.8)

0=\sum_{r+s+t=n}m_{r+1+t}^{A}\bullet_{r+1}m_{s}^{A}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:infinitesimal_composition}}}{{=}}\sum_{r+s+t=n}(-1)^{rs+t}m_{r+1+t}^{A}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A}^{\otimes r}\otimes m_{s}^{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}^{\otimes t}),

where
n\geq 1
and the sum ranges over all ordered partitions
r+s+t=n
with

s\geq 1
and
r,t\geq 0
. This is the form of the
A_{\infty}
-equations used in

[
LH03
, Definition 1.2.1.1]
.

5.9
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra. According to

eq.

5.4
, the components of the
A_{\infty}
-equation

{m^{A}}\left\{m^{A}\right\}=0
are

(5.10)

\displaystyle\begin{split}0&=\sum_{p+q-1=n}\sum_{i=1}^{p}m_{p}^{A}\bullet_{i}m_{q}^{A}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:infinitesimal_composition}}}{{=}}\sum_{p+q-1=n}\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{p+q+i(q+1)}m_{p}^{A}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A}^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes m_{q}^{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}^{\otimes(n-i)})\\
&=\sum_{p+q-1=n}\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{1+n+i(q+1)}m_{p}^{A}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A}^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes m_{q}^{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}^{\otimes(n-i)})\\
&=\sum_{p+q-1=n}\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{(i-1)(q+1)+n-q}m_{p}^{A}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A}^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes m_{q}^{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}^{\otimes(n-i)}),\\
\end{split}

where the first sum ranges over all ordered decompositions
p+q-1=n
with

p,q\geq 1
, see
5.2
. The last line in

eq.

5.10
is the form of the
A_{\infty}
-equations
used in
[
Tra08
, Proposition 1.4]
.

5.11
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Let us review the meaning of the

A_{\infty}
-equations for small values of
n
. We remind the reader of

Example

5.1
.

(1)

The
A_{\infty}
-equation for
n=1
is

0=m_{1}^{A}\bullet_{1}m_{1}^{A},

so that
(A,m_{1}^{A})
is a dg vector space. Below we write

\partial=\partial_{A,A}
for the differential of the dg vector space

\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left((A,m_{1}^{A}),(A,m_{1}^{A})\right)
.

(2)

The
A_{\infty}
-equation for
n=2
is

0=m_{1}^{A}\bullet_{1}m_{2}^{A}+m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{1}m_{1}^{A}+m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{2}m_{1}^{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ex:partial-via-braces}}}{{=}}\partial(m_{2}^{A}),

so that

m_{2}^{A}\colon(A,m_{1}^{A})\otimes(A,m_{1}^{A})\longrightarrow(A,m_{1}^{A}),

is a morphism of dg vector spaces.

(3)

The
A_{\infty}
-equation for
n=3
is

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\overbrace{m_{1}^{A}\bullet_{1}m_{3}^{A}+m_{3}^{A}\bullet_{1}m_{1}^{A}+m_{3}^{A}\bullet_{2}m_{1}^{A}+m_{3}^{A}\bullet_{3}m_{1}^{A}}^{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{ex:partial-via-braces}}}{{=}}\partial(m_{3}^{A})}

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{1}m_{2}^{A}+m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{2}m_{2}^{A},

which, using
eqs.

3.28
and
3.33
and

Example

5.1
, can written as

(5.12)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\partial(m_{3}^{A})+m_{2}^{A}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes m_{2}^{A}-m_{2}^{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}),

so that
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A,m_{1}^{A}\right)
is a (non-unital) associative graded algebra.
Notice also that, if
\partial(m_{3}^{A})=0
, then the binary multiplication

m_{2}^{A}
is already associative (for example if
m_{1}^{A}=0
or

m_{3}^{A}=0
). In particular, a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure is
equipped with a (non-unital) associative multiplication.

In general, using the formula in the penultimate line
in
eq.

5.10
and
Example

5.1
, we
see that the
A_{\infty}
-equation for
n\geq 1
can be also be written as

(5.13)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\partial(m_{n}^{A})&=-\sum_{p+q-1=n}{m_{p}^{A}}\left\{m_{q}^{A}\right\}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:A-infty-equations-operadic}}}{{=}}\sum_{p+q-1=n}\sum_{i=1}^{p}(-1)^{i(q+1)+n}m_{p}^{A}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A}^{\otimes(i-1)}\otimes m_{q}^{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A}^{\otimes(n-i)}),\end{split}

where the first sum ranges over all ordered decompositions
p+q-1=n
with

p,q\geq 2
.
Equation

5.13
is the form of the

A_{\infty}
-equations used in
[
Mar06
]
.

5.14
.

It follows from
Remark

5.11
that an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
(A,m^{A})

with
m_{n}^{A}=0
for
n\geq 3
is a dg algebra. Conversely, a dg algebra gives
rise to an
A_{\infty}
-algebra with
m_{3}^{A}=0
for
n\geq 0
. In this way we
may and we will identify dg algebras with
A_{\infty}
-algebras whose operations
of arity
3
or higher vanish.

We now discuss the
A_{\infty}
-equations in the case of minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebras.

5.15
.

For a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra
(A,m^{A})
, we let

\overline{m}^{A}\coloneqq m^{A}-m_{2}^{A}=(0,0,m_{3}^{A},m_{4}^{A},\dots,m_{n}^{A},\dots)\in\prod_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{C}^{n,2-n}\!\left(A\right).

We also write
(A,\overline{m}^{A})=(A,m^{A})
when we wish to view
A
as being
equipped with the associative algebra structure given by
m_{2}^{A}
, rather
than as a mere graded vector space.

5.16
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra. The
A_{\infty}
-equation for

n=1,2
are trivial and for
n\geq 3
they take the form

(5.17)

0=\sum_{p+q-1=n}{m_{p}^{A}}\left\{m_{q}^{A}\right\},

where the sum ranges over all ordered decompositions
p+q-1=n
with
p,q,\geq 2
. In particular, the
A_{\infty}
-equation for
n=3
is simply the
associativity of the binary operation:

{m_{2}^{A}}\left\{m_{2}^{A}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Ai-associativity}}}{{=}}m_{2}^{A}\circ(\mathrm{id}_{A}\otimes m_{2}^{A}-m_{2}^{A}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{A})=0.

5.18
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Observe that
20
20

20

Here we
use the fact that the brace relations for the Hochschild cochain complex
extend to its natural completion with respect to the arity degree.

\displaystyle{m^{A}}\left\{m^{A}\right\}=\Sq(m^{A})

\displaystyle=\Sq(m_{2}^{A}+\overline{m}^{A})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Gerstehaber_square-relations}}}{{=}}\underbrace{\Sq(m_{2}^{A})}_{=0}+\Sq(\overline{m}^{A})+[m_{2}^{A},\overline{m}^{A}]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Hd}}}{{=}}d_{\mathrm{Hoch}}(\overline{m}^{A})+\Sq(\overline{m}^{A}),

where
\Sq(m_{2}^{A})=0
since the multiplication
m_{2}^{A}
is associative. In
summary, the equation of a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra is the

Maurer–Cartan equation
21
21

21

If
\chark{\mathbf{k}}\neq 2
,
the Maurer–Cartan equation takes the more familiar form

d_{\mathrm{Hoch}}(\overline{m}^{A})+\tfrac{1}{2}[\overline{m}^{A},\overline{m}^{A}]=0,

see
Remark

3.14
.

(5.19)

d_{\mathrm{Hoch}}(\overline{m}^{A})+\Sq(\overline{m}^{A})=0,

where the Hochschild differential is the one for the associative algebra

(A,m_{2}^{A})
.

5.20
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
and
(B,m^{B})
be
A_{\infty}
-algebras. An

A_{\infty}
-morphism

f\colon(A,m^{A})\to(B,m^{B})
is a tuple

(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},\dots,f_{n},\dots)\in\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}^{1-n}\!\left(A^{\otimes n},B\right)

such that the
A_{\infty}
-morphism equations
hold for each
n\geq 1
:

(5.21)

\sum_{p+q-1=n}{f_{p}}\left\{m_{q}^{A}\right\}=\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum{m_{r}^{B}}\left\{f_{i_{1}},\dots,f_{i_{r}}\right\},

where the sum on the left-hand side runs over all ordered decompositions

p+q-1=n
with
p,q\geq 1
, the internal sum on the right-hand side ranges
over all ordered decompositions
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}=r
, and

(5.22)

{m_{r}^{B}}\left\{f_{i_{1}},\dots,f_{i_{r}}\right\}\coloneqq\\
(\cdots((m_{r}^{B}\bullet_{1}f_{i_{1}})\bullet_{i_{1}+1}f_{i_{2}})\cdots)\bullet_{i_{1}+i_{2}+\cdots+i_{r-1}+1}f_{i_{r}}.

The degree
0
morphism
f_{1}\colon A\to B
is called the
linear part

of
f
. By the
A_{\infty}
-morphism equation for
n=1
,

f_{1}\bullet_{1}m_{1}^{A}=m_{1}^{B}\bullet_{1}f_{1},

so that
f_{1}\colon(A,m_{1}^{A})\to(B,m_{1}^{B})
is a morphism of dg vector
spaces. An
A_{\infty}
-morphism
f
as above is

•

an
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism
if the linear part
f_{1}
is an
isomorphism of graded vector spaces.

•

a
gauge
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism
if the linear part
f_{1}
is the
identity (hence we must have
A=B
).

•

an
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism
if the linear part
f_{1}
is an
quasi-isomorphism of dg vector spaces.

•

strict
if
f_{n}=0
for
n\geq 2
.

5.23
.

Every
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism between
A_{\infty}
-algebras admits an
inverse up to a suitable notion of homotopy, see for
example
[
Sei08
, Theorem 2.9]
.

5.24
.

Every
A_{\infty}
-algebra (for example a dg algebra) is

A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphic to a minimal one, called a minimal model, which is
unique up to non-unique
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism,
see
[
Mer99
,
KS01
,
Mar04
,
Mar06
]
among many others. We review the
construction of minimal models of dg algebras in
Section

5.6
.

5.25
.

Let
f\colon(A,m^{A})\to(B,m^{B})
be a strict
A_{\infty}
-morphism between

A_{\infty}
-algebras. In this case, the
A_{\infty}
-morphism equations simplify
to

(5.26)

\displaystyle\begin{split}f_{1}\circ m_{n}^{A}&=(\cdots((m_{n}^{A}\bullet_{1}f_{1})\bullet_{2}f_{1})\cdots)\bullet_{n}f_{n}\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:infinitesimal_composition}}}{{=}}m_{n}^{B}\circ(\underbrace{f_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{1}}_{n\text{ times}}),\end{split}

so that
f_{1}\colon A\to B
is strictly compatible with the corresponding

A_{\infty}
-structures.

5.27
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
f_{1}\colon A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}B
an isomorphism of graded vector spaces. It
follows from
eq.

5.26
that
f_{1}
determines a strict

A_{\infty}
-isomorphism if we endow
B
with the
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure

m_{n}^{B}\coloneqq f_{1}\circ(m_{n}^{A}\circ(f_{1}^{-1}\otimes\cdots\otimes f_{1}^{-1})),\qquad n\geq 1,

which is of course uniquely determined.
More generally,
any
tuple of morphisms

f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},\dots,f_{n},\dots)\in\prod_{n\geq 1}\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}^{1-n}\!\left(A^{\otimes n},B\right)

such that
f_{1}
is an isomorphism of graded vector spaces determines a unique

A_{\infty}
-algebra structure
m^{B}
on
B
such that

f\colon(A,m^{A})\to(B,m^{B})
is an
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism (the claimed

A_{\infty}
-structure on
B
is constructed inductively by taking the

A_{\infty}
-morphism equations as an Ansatz). This flexibility of

A_{\infty}
-algebra structures is both a curse and a blessing: On the positive
side, the notion of
A_{\infty}
-algebra is invariant under homotopy equivalences
of dg vector spaces
[
Mar04
]
while that of dg algebra is not; on the
negative side, it is very difficult to recognise
A_{\infty}
-isomorphic

A_{\infty}
-algebras and to define useful invariants of these in terms of their
higher operations.

5.28
.

Let
f\colon(A,m^{A})\to(B,m^{B})
be an
A_{\infty}
-morphism between
minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebras. The
A_{\infty}
-morphism equation for
n=2

simplifies to

f_{1}\bullet_{1}m_{2}^{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:binary_brace}}}{{=}}{f_{1}}\left\{m_{2}^{A}\right\}={m_{2}^{B}}\left\{f_{1},f_{1}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:morphism-brace}}}{{=}}(m_{2}^{B}\bullet_{1}f_{1})\bullet_{2}f_{2}.

Explicitly,

f_{1}(m_{2}^{A}(x,y))=m_{2}^{B}(f_{1}(x),f_{1}(y)),\qquad x,y\in A,

so that
f_{1}\colon A\to B
is a morphism of (non-unital) associative algebras.

In this article we are chiefly interested in
A_{\infty}
-algebras that are sparse
in the following sense.

5.29
.

Let
d\geq 1
. A graded vector space
V
is
d
-sparse
if
V^{i}\neq 0

implies
i\in d\mathbb{Z}
(this conditions is of course vacuous for
d=1
). We say
that an
A_{\infty}
-algebra is
d
-sparse
if its underlying graded
vector space is
d
-sparse. Similarly, we say that an
A_{\infty}
-algebra (for
example a dg algebra) is
cohomologically
d
-sparse
if its cohomology
(with respect to
m_{1}
) is a
d
-sparse graded vector space.

5.30
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be a
d
-sparse
A_{\infty}
-algebra. For degree reasons, if

n\geq 1
and
m_{n}^{A}\neq 0
, then we must have
n\in 2+d\mathbb{Z}
(recall that

m_{n}^{A}
has degree
2-n
), see for example
[
JKM22
, Proposition 4.3.4]
.
Thus, the only possibly non-zero operations in such a
d
-sparse

A_{\infty}
-algebra are
m_{1}^{A}
(if
d=1
) and
m_{kd+2}^{A}
,
k\geq 0
. In
particular,
d
-sparse
A_{\infty}
-algebras with
d\geq 2
are necessarily
minimal (but cohomologically
d
-sparse ones need not be).

5.31
.

Let
f\colon(A,m^{A})\to(B,m^{B})
be an
A_{\infty}
-morphism between

d
-sparse algebras,
d\geq 2
. For degree reasons,
f_{n}\neq 0
then we must
have
n\in 1+d\mathbb{Z}
(recall that
f_{n}
has degree
1-n
), see for
example
[
JKM22
, Proposition 4.4.4]
. Thus, the only possible non-zero
components of a morphism between such
d
-sparse algebras are
f_{kd+1}
,

k\geq 0
.

5.3.
A_{\infty}
-bimodules and
A_{\infty}
-morphisms between them

We remind the reader of
5.2
.

5.32
.

Let
A=(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra. An
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over

A
is a pair
(M,m^{M})
consisting of a graded vector space
M
and a
cochain

m^{M}=(m_{1}^{M},m_{2}^{M},m_{3}^{M},\dots,m_{n}^{M},\dots)\in\prod_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{n-1,2-n}\!\left(M,M\right)

with

m_{n}^{M}=(m_{p,q}^{M}\colon A^{\otimes p}\otimes M\otimes A^{\otimes q}\longrightarrow M)_{p+1+q=n},

called an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure
, such that the

A_{\infty}
-bimodule equation
holds:

{m^{M}}\left\{m^{A}\right\}+{m^{M}}\left\{m^{M}\right\}=0.

Notice that, if
p+1+q=n
, then the cochain
m_{p,q}^{M}
has the vertical degree
2-n=1-p-q
.

5.33
.

Under the name ‘bipolydule’, a slightly implicit definition of

A_{\infty}
-bimodule is given in
[
LH03
, Section 2.5.1]
where the
corresponding derived category is also constructed. Explicit (equivalent)
definitions of
A_{\infty}
-bimodule including the complete set of equations are
given in
[
Mar92
, Section 1.10]
and
[
Tra08
, Section 2]
, see also

[
GJ90
]
where the definition is given using the formalism of dg
bicomodules over dg bicoalgebras (also used in
[
Tra08
]
). An operadic
approach to the derived category of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules can extracted from
the very general results in
[
BM09
]
. The more general notion of

A_{\infty}
-bimodule over an
A_{\infty}
-category is studied in
[
LM08
]
.

5.34
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{A})
an

A_{\infty}
-bimodule. According to the conventions in

5.2
, the components of the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule
equation can be written as

(5.35)

\displaystyle 0=\sum_{p+q+s=n}{m_{p,q}^{M}}\left\{m_{s}^{A}\right\}+\sum_{p+q+s+t+1=n}m_{p,q}^{M}\bullet_{p+1}m_{s,t}^{M},\qquad n\geq 1,

where the sum on the left term ranges over all ordered decompositions

p+q+s=n
with
s\geq 1
and
p+q\geq 1
, and the sum on the right term ranges
over all ordered decompositions
p+q+s+t+1=n
with
p,q,s,t\geq 0
. The left
term decomposes further as

\sum_{p+q+s=n}{m_{p,q}^{M}}\left\{m_{s}^{A}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:infinitesimal_composition}}}{{=}}\sum_{p+q+s=n}\sum_{i=1}^{p}m_{p,q}^{M}\bullet_{i}m_{s}^{A}+\sum_{p+q+s=n}\sum_{j=1}^{q}m_{p,q}^{M}\bullet_{p+1+j}m^{A},

compare with the form of the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule equations given
in
[
Tra08
, Proposition 2.7]
. Notice also that, for a fixed value of
n
,
the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule equation (
5.35
) in fact encodes a
system of
n
linearly independent equations, for it takes place in the vector
space

\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{n-1,2-n}\!\left(A,M\right)=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{i-1,n-i,2-n}\!\left(A,M\right).

In this article we are mainly interested in the following canonical

A_{\infty}
-bimodule.

Definition-Proposition 5.36

(
[
Tra08
, Lemma 4.1]
)
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra. The
diagonal

A_{\infty}
-bimodule

(A(0),m^{A[0]})
is the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule with
underlying graded vector space
A
and
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure with
operations
22
22

22

We denote the diagonal
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure by

m^{A[0]}
to avoid confusing it with the given
A_{\infty}
-algebra
structure
m^{A}
on
A
.

m_{n}^{A[0]}=(m_{p,q}^{A[0]})_{p+1+q=n}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{n-1,2-n}\!\left(A,A\right),\qquad n\geq 1,

where

m_{p,q}^{A[0]}\coloneqq m_{p+1+q}^{A},\qquad p+1+q=n.

We remind the reader of the following elementary fact: Given an algebra
A
and
a vector space
M
, the set of
A
-bimodule structures on
M
is in bijection
with the set of algebra structures on the vector space
A\oplus M
that extend
the given algebra structure on
A
and such that
M\subseteq A\oplus M
is a
square-zero ideal. This observation admits the following generalisation to

A_{\infty}
-algebras and
A_{\infty}
-bimodules.

5.37
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
M
a graded vector space. The
association
m^{M}\mapsto m^{A}+m^{M}
induces bijections between the
following:

(1)

The set of
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structures
m^{M}
over
(A,m^{A})
.

(2)

The set of cochains

m^{A\ltimes M}=m^{A}+m^{M}\in\prod_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{C}^{n,2-n}\!\left(A\,|\,M\right)

such that
{m^{A\ltimes M}}\left\{m^{A\ltimes M}\right\}=0
, that is such
that
(A\oplus M,m^{A\ltimes M})
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra.

Proof.

Recall that
{m^{A}}\left\{m^{A}\right\}=0
since
m^{A}
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
structure. Hence, given a cochain

m^{M}\in\prod_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{C}^{n,2-n}\!\left(A\,|\,M\right),

for
m^{A\ltimes M}=m^{A}+m^{M}
we have

{m^{A\ltimes M}}\left\{m^{A\ltimes}\right\}=\underbrace{{m^{A}}\left\{m^{A}\right\}}_{=0}+\underbrace{{m^{A}}\left\{m^{M}\right\}}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:infinitesimal_composition}}}{{=}}0}+{m^{M}}\left\{m^{A}\right\}+{m^{M}}\left\{m^{M}\right\}.

The claim follows from the above equation together with the observation that
there is an inclusion of bigraded vector spaces

\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\,|\,M\right)\subseteq\operatorname{C}^{\bullet,*}\!\left(A\oplus M\right)
that is compatible with the infinitesimal
compositions.
∎

Proposition

5.37
motivates the following notation.

5.38
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{A})
an

A_{\infty}
-bimodule. We set

m^{A\ltimes M}\coloneqq m^{A}+m^{M}\in\prod_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{C}^{n,*}\!\left(A\,|\,M\right).

5.39
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{M})
an

A_{\infty}
-bimodule over it. Notice that the associated
A_{\infty}
-algebra

(A\oplus M,m^{A\ltimes M})
is also minimal. It follows from

Remark

5.16
that the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule equations
for
n=1,2
are trivial, and for
n=3
they yield the fact that the
square-zero product
m_{2}^{A\ltimes M}
is associative, so that
M
is a
(non-unital) graded
A
-bimodule in the associative sense.

The upshot of
Proposition

5.37
is that we can reduce
several aspects of the theory of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules to that of

A_{\infty}
-algebras (with appropriate modifications). For example, the notion of
morphism between
A_{\infty}
-bimodules can be formulated in the following manner.
We remind the reader of
Remark

4.47
.

5.40
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{M})
and
(N,m^{N})

two
A_{\infty}
-bimodules over it. An
A_{\infty}
-morphism of

A_{\infty}
-bimodules

{f\colon(M,m^{M})\to(N,m^{N})}
is a cochain

f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},\dots,f_{n},\dots)\in\prod_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{n-1,1-n}\!\left(M,N\right)

such that

\widetilde{f}\coloneqq\mathrm{id}_{A}\oplus f\colon(A\oplus M,m^{A\ltimes M})\to(A\oplus N,m^{A\ltimes N})

is a morphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras; notice that the cochain
f_{n}
is in fact a tuple

f_{n}=(f_{p,q}\colon A^{\otimes p}\otimes M\otimes A^{\otimes q}\longrightarrow N)_{p+1+q=n},

of homogeneous morphisms of vector spaces if (vertical) degree
1-n=-p-q
.
The degree
0
morphisms
f_{1}=f_{0,0}\colon M\to N
is called the
linear part

of
f
. By the
A_{\infty}
-morphism equation for
n=1
,

f_{1}\bullet_{1}m_{1}^{M}=m_{1}^{n}\bullet_{1}f_{1},

so that
f_{1}\colon(M,m_{1}^{M})\to(N,m_{1}^{N})
is a morphism of dg vector
spaces. An
A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules
f
as above is

•

an
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism
if the linear part
f_{1}
is an
isomorphism of graded vector spaces.

•

a
gauge
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism
if the linear part
f_{1}
is the
identity (hence we must have
M=N
).

•

an
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism
if the linear part
f_{1}
is an
quasi-isomorphism of dg vector spaces.

•

strict
if
f_{n}=0
for
n\geq 2
.

5.41
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra and

f\colon(M,m^{M})\to(N,m^{N})
an
A_{\infty}
-morphism between
minimal
A_{\infty}
-bimodules over it. It follows from

Remark

5.28
that

\widetilde{f}=\mathrm{id}_{A}\oplus f_{1}\colon(A\oplus M,m^{A\ltimes M})\longrightarrow(A\oplus N,m^{A\ltimes N})

is a morphism of (non-unital) associative algebras. Equivalently,

f_{1}\colon(M,m^{M})\longrightarrow(N,m^{N})

is a morphism of (non-unital) associative graded
A
-bimodules.

5.42
.

Every
A_{\infty}
-bimodule is
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphic to a minimal one,
called a minimal model, which is unique up to non-unique

A_{\infty}
-isomorphism. This follows from the existence of the projective
model structure on dg operads, since the bimodule Hochschild complex arises
from the linear endomorphism operad from
[
BM09
]
. In
Section

5.6

we explain how to use
Proposition

5.37
together with the
well-known formulas for transferred
A_{\infty}
-algebra structures
[
Mar06
]

to obtain explicit formulas in the case of dg bimodules over dg algebras.

In this article we are chiefly interested in sparse minimal

A_{\infty}
-bimodules, compare with
Definition

5.29
.

5.43
.

Let
d\geq 1
. An
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over an
A_{\infty}
-algebra is

d
-sparse
if its underlying graded vector space is
d
-sparse (this
condition is vacuous if
d=1
). More generally, an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule (for
example a dg bimodule) is

cohomologically
d
-sparse
if its cohomology (with respect to
m_{1}
) is
a
d
-sparse graded vector space.

5.44
.

Clearly,
Remarks

5.30
and
5.31
apply equally well
to
d
-sparse
A_{\infty}
-bimodules and morphisms between them. In particular,
the first possibly non-trivial higher operation in a
d
-sparse

A_{\infty}
-bimodule is

m_{d+2}=(m_{p,q})_{p+1+q=d+2}.

5.4.
Shifts and linear duals of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

We now turn our attention to shifts and linear duals of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules.
We recall the relevant definitions.

Definition-Proposition 5.45
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{M})
an

A_{\infty}
-bimodule over it.
Given
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the graded vector space

M(n)
carries a canonical
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure with operations

m_{k}^{M(n)}\coloneqq(m_{p,q}^{M(n)})_{p+1+q=k}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{k-1,2-k}\!\left(M(n),M(n)\right),\qquad k\geq 1,

where

m_{p,q}^{M(n)}\coloneqq(m_{p,q}^{M})^{(n)},\qquad p+1+q=k,

is defined by the formula in
eq.

4.48
.

Proof.

The fact that
m^{M(n)}
is an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure follows from

Proposition

4.51
, as the latter yields the fact that

m^{A\ltimes M(n)}=m^{A}+m^{M(n)}
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure
that satisfies the conditions in
Proposition

5.37
:

{m^{A\ltimes M(n)}}\left\{m^{A\ltimes M(n)}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Psi}}}{{=}}{\Psi(m^{A\ltimes M})}\left\{\Psi(m^{A\ltimes M})\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {prop:shifting-cochains}}}{{=}}\Psi(\underbrace{{m^{A\ltimes M}}\left\{m^{A\ltimes M}\right\}}_{=0})=0.\qed

5.46
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{M})
a cohomologically

d
-sparse
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over it,
d\geq 1
. Clearly,
(M(n),m^{M(n)})
is
cohomologically
d
-sparse if and only if
n\in d\mathbb{Z}
.

The following basic result can be deduced, using
[
LM08
, Section 3.8]
, from
the desription of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules as suitable
A_{\infty}
-functors with
values in the dg category of dg vector spaces given in
op. cit.
We give a
direct proof for the convenience of the reader.

5.47
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{M})
and
(N,m^{N})

two
A_{\infty}
-bimodules over it. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and suppose given an

A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

\varphi\colon(M,m^{M})\longrightarrow(N,m^{N}).

Then, the degree
-p-q
maps

\varphi_{p,q}^{(n)}=\Psi(\varphi_{p,q})\colon A^{\otimes p}\otimes M(n)\otimes A^{\otimes q}\longrightarrow N(n),\qquad k\geq 1,\qquad p+1+q=k,

defined as in
4.53
, assemble into an

A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

\varphi^{(n)}\colon(M(n),m^{M(n)})\longrightarrow(N(n),m^{N(n)}).

Proof.

We set

\widetilde{\varphi}\coloneq\mathrm{id}_{A}\oplus\varphi\colon(A\oplus M,m^{A\ltimes M})\longrightarrow(A\oplus N,m^{A\ltimes N}),

which is a
A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras by assumption.
Similarly, we set

\widetilde{\varphi}(n)\coloneq\mathrm{id}_{A}\oplus\varphi(n)\colon(A\oplus M(n),m^{A\ltimes M(n)})\longrightarrow(A\oplus N(n),m^{A\ltimes N(n)}).

Using the compatibilities stated in
4.53
, we
compute, for
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{p+q-1=n}{\widetilde{\varphi}_{p}(n)}\left\{m_{q}^{A\ltimes M(n)}\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p+q-1=n}{\Psi(\widetilde{\varphi}_{p})}\left\{\Psi(m_{q}^{A\ltimes M})\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{p+q-1=n}\Psi({\widetilde{\varphi}_{p}}\left\{m_{q}^{A\ltimes M}\right\})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Ai-morphism}}}{{=}}\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum\Psi({m_{r}^{A\ltimes M}}\left\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{i_{1}},\dots,\widetilde{\varphi}_{i_{r}}\right\})

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum{\Psi(m_{r}^{A\ltimes M})}\left\{\Psi(\widetilde{\varphi}_{i_{1}}),\dots,\Psi(\widetilde{\varphi}_{i_{r}})\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=1}^{n}\sum{m_{r}^{A\ltimes M(n)}}\left\{\widetilde{\varphi}_{i_{1}}(n),\dots,\widetilde{\varphi}_{i_{r}}(n)\right\},

where all the non-indexed internal sums range over all ordered
decompositions
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}=n
. This shows that
\widetilde{\varphi}(n)
is
a morphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras, as required.
∎

Definition-Proposition 5.48

(
[
Tra08
, Lemma 2.9]
)
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{M})
an

A_{\infty}
-bimodule over it. The linear dual
DM=\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(M,\mathbf{k}\right)
carries
a canonical
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure with operations

m_{n}^{DM}\coloneqq(m_{p,q}^{DM})_{p+1+q=n}\in\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{n-1,2-n}\!\left(DM,DM\right),\qquad n\geq 1,

where

m_{p,q}^{DM}\coloneqq(m_{q,p}^{M})^{D},\qquad p+1+q=n,

is defined by the formula in
eq.

4.54
. In particular, the differential

m_{1}^{DM}
on
DM
is given, in terms of the differential
m_{1}^{M}
on

M
, by the expected formula

m_{1}^{DM}(f)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:cD}}}{{=}}(-1)^{(0+1)(0+1)+|f|\lvert m_{1}^{M}\rvert_{\mathrm{v}}}f\circ m_{1}^{M}=(-1)^{|f|+1}f\circ m_{1}^{M}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {ex:DV}}}{{=}}\partial_{M,\mathbf{k}}(f).

Proof.

The fact that
m^{DM}
is an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure follows from

Proposition

4.58
, as the latter yields the fact that

m^{A\ltimes DM}=m^{A}+m^{DM}
is an
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure that
satisfies the conditions in
Proposition

5.37
:

{m^{A\ltimes DM}}\left\{m^{A\ltimes DM}\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:Theta}}}{{=}}{\Theta(m^{A\ltimes M})}\left\{\Theta(m^{A\ltimes M})\right\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {prop:dualising-cochains}}}{{=}}\Theta(\underbrace{{m^{A\ltimes M}}\left\{m^{A\ltimes M}\right\}}_{=0})=0.\qed

5.49
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{M})
a cohomologically

d
-sparse
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over it,
d\geq 1
. Clearly,
(DM,m^{DM})
is
also cohomologically
d
-sparse.

5.50
.

The attentive reader will notice, when comparing the formula in
eq.

4.54

that defines the cochain
m_{p,q}^{DM}
in
5.48

with the corresponding formula in
[
Tra08
, Lemma 2.9]
, that the signs are
off by the factor
(-1)^{(p+1)(q+1)}
. Indeed, this missing factor has been
added in the erratum
[
Tra11
]
to
op. cit.

5.51
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{M})
and
(N,m^{N})

two
A_{\infty}
-bimodules over it. Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and suppose given an

A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

\varphi\colon(M,m^{M})\longrightarrow(N,m^{N}).

Then, the degree
-p-q
maps

\varphi_{p,q}^{D}=\Theta(\varphi_{p,q})\colon A^{\otimes p}\otimes DN\otimes A^{\otimes q}\longrightarrow DN,\qquad k\geq 1,\qquad p+1+q=k,

defined as in
4.60
, assemble into an
A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

\varphi^{D}\colon(DN,m^{DN})\longrightarrow(DM,m^{DM}).

Proof.

Recall that
|\varphi|=1
and
|m|=2
. Using the compatibilities stated
in
4.60
, we compute, for
p,q\geq 0
,

\displaystyle{\Theta(\varphi_{p,q})}\left\{m_{s}^{A}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:binary_brace}}}{{=}}\Theta({\varphi_{p,q}}\left\{m_{s}^{A}\right\})

\displaystyle{\Theta(\varphi_{p,q})}\left\{m_{t,s}^{DN}\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:binary_brace}}}{{=}}\Theta(\varphi_{p,q})\bullet_{q+1}\Theta(m_{s,t}^{N})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{lemma:Theta}}}{{=}}-\Theta({m_{t,s}^{N}}\left\{\varphi_{p,q}\right\})

\displaystyle=-\Theta({m_{s,t}^{N}}\left\{\mathrm{id}_{A},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\dots}},\mathrm{id}_{A},\varphi_{p,q},\mathrm{id}_{A},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t}}{{\dots}},\mathrm{id}_{A}\right\}).

Similarly,

\displaystyle{\Theta(m_{t,s}^{DM})}\left\{\mathrm{id}_{A},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle q}}{{\dots}},\mathrm{id}_{A},\Theta(\varphi_{p,q}),\mathrm{id}_{A},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\dots}},\mathrm{id}_{A}\right\}

\displaystyle={\Theta(m_{t,s}^{DM})}\left\{\Theta(\varphi_{p,q})\right\}

\displaystyle=-\Theta({\varphi_{p,q}}\left\{m_{s,t}^{M}\right\}).

Thanks to the additional minus signs, we see that applying
\Theta
to the

A_{\infty}
-morphism equation satisfied by
\varphi
yields the

A_{\infty}
-morphism equation for
\varphi^{D}=\Theta(\varphi)
. This finishes
the proof.
∎

We establish certain compatibilities between shifts and linear duals that are
needed in the sequel. These are rather elementary, but we did not find
suitable references in the literature. We remind the reader of

4.65
.

5.52
.

Let
(A,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-algebra and
(M,m^{M})
an

A_{\infty}
-bimodule over it. Fix
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. The following statements hold:

(1)

The map

\alpha\colon(DM)(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D(M(-n)),\qquad\mathbf{s}^{n}f\longmapsto(-1)^{n|f|}f\mathbf{s}^{n},

see
Example

2.33
, yields a strict
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism of

A_{\infty}
-bimodules

\alpha\colon((DM)(n),m^{(DM)[-n]})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(D(M(-n)),m^{D(M(-n))}).

(2)

Suppose
(M,m^{M})
is minimal and that there
exists an isomorphism of graded
A
-bimodules

\varphi\colon M(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}DM.

Then, the isomorphism of graded
A
-bimodules

\psi\colon M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}D(M(n)),\qquad m\longmapsto(-1)^{n|m|}\varphi(\mathbf{s}^{n}m)\mathbf{s}^{-n},

yields a strict
A_{\infty}
-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

\psi\colon(M,\Upsilon(m^{M}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(D(M(n)),m^{D(M(n))}),

compare with
Remark

4.64
.

Proof.

(
1
) Let
k\geq 1
and
p+1+q=k
. For

x_{1},\dots,x_{p},y_{1},\dots,y_{q}\in A
,
f\in DM
and
m\in M
, on the one hand,

\displaystyle m_{q,p}^{D(M(-n))}

\displaystyle(y_{1},\dots,y_{q},\alpha(\mathbf{s}^{n}f),x_{1},\dots,x_{p})(\mathbf{s}^{-n}m)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:cD}}}{{=}}(-1)^{\maltese_{1}}\alpha(\mathbf{s}^{n}f)(m_{p,q}^{M[-n]}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},\mathbf{s}^{-n}m,y_{1},\dots,y_{q}))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\maltese_{1}+n|f|}f(\mathbf{s}^{n}(m_{p,q}^{M[-n]}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},\mathbf{s}^{-n}m,y_{1},\dots,y_{q}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:cn}}}{{=}}(-1)^{\maltese_{1}+n|f|+\maltese_{2}}f(\mathbf{s}^{n}(\mathbf{s}^{-n}m_{p,q}^{M}(y_{1},\dots,y_{q},m,y_{1},\dots,y_{q})))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\maltese_{3}}f(m_{p,q}^{M}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},m,y_{1},\dots,y_{q})),

where

\displaystyle\maltese_{1}

\displaystyle=\textstyle(p+1)(q+1)+(|f|-n)(p+1+q)

\displaystyle\textstyle\phantom{=}+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{q}|y_{j}|\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{p}|x_{i}|+|f|+|m|\right)

\displaystyle\maltese_{2}

\displaystyle=\textstyle n\left(p+1+q+\sum_{i=1}^{p}|x_{i}|\right)

\displaystyle\maltese_{3}

\displaystyle=\textstyle(p+1)(q+1)+|f|(p+1+q)+n\left(|f|+\sum_{i=1}^{p}|x_{i}|\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle\phantom{=}+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{q}|y_{j}|\right)\left(|f|+\sum_{i=1}^{p}|x_{i}|+|m|\right).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\alpha(m_{q,p}^{(DM)(n)})

\displaystyle(y_{1},\dots,y_{q},\mathbf{s}^{n}f,x_{1},\dots,x_{p}))(\mathbf{s}^{-n}m)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:cn}}}{{=}}(-1)^{\maltese_{4}}\alpha(\mathbf{s}^{n}m_{q,p}^{DM}(y_{1},\dots,y_{q},f,x_{1},\dots,x_{p}))(\mathbf{s}^{-n}m)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\maltese_{4}+\maltese_{5}}(m_{q,p}^{DM}(y_{1},\dots,y_{q},f,x_{1},\dots,x_{p})\mathbf{s}^{n})(\mathbf{s}^{-n}m)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\maltese_{4}+\maltese_{5}}(m_{q,p}^{DM}(y_{1},\dots,y_{q},f,x_{1},\dots,x_{p}))(m)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:cD}}}{{=}}(-1)^{\maltese_{4}+\maltese_{5}+\maltese_{6}}f(m_{p,q}^{M}(x_{1},\dots,x_{p},m,y_{1},\dots,y_{q})),

where

\displaystyle\maltese_{4}

\displaystyle=\textstyle n\left(p+1+q+\sum_{j=1}^{q}|y_{j}|\right)

\displaystyle\maltese_{5}

\displaystyle=\textstyle n(p+1+q+\sum_{j=1}^{q}|y_{j}|+|f|+\sum_{i=1}^{p}|x_{i}|)

\displaystyle\maltese_{6}

\displaystyle=\textstyle(p+1)(q+1)+|f|(p+1+q)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\textstyle+\left(\sum_{j=1}^{q}|y_{j}|\right)\left(|f|+\sum_{i=1}^{p}|x_{i}|+|m|\right).

Since
\maltese_{3}=\maltese_{4}+\maltese_{5}+\maltese_{6}
, the claim follows.

(
2
) Using and
5.45
and

5.48
to define the induced
A_{\infty}
-structure on
the
n
-fold shift and the linear dual of
M
, respectively, this is immediate
from the construction of
\Upsilon
given in
4.65
, compare
with
Remarks

4.30
and
5.27
.
∎

5.5.
Restriction of scalars

We now study the operation of restriction of scalars for
A_{\infty}
-bimodules and
its compatibility with the passage to shifts and linear duals. Let
(A,m^{A})

and
(B,m^{B})
be
A_{\infty}
-algebras and fix an
A_{\infty}
-morphism of

A_{\infty}
-algebras

\varphi\colon(A,m^{A})\longrightarrow(B,m^{B}).

Definition-Proposition 5.53

(
cf.
[
Kel01
, Section 6.2]
)
.

Let
(M,m^{M})
be an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
(B,m^{B})
. For

p,q\geq 0
and
n\coloneqq p+1+q
, we define

m_{p,q}^{\varphi^{*}M}\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{r+1+s=k}\sum{m_{r,s}^{M}}\left\{\varphi_{i_{1}},\dots,\varphi_{i_{r}},\mathrm{id}_{M},\varphi_{j_{1}},\dots,\varphi_{j_{s}}\right\},

where the middle internal sum ranges over all ordered decompositions
r+1+s=n

with
r,s\geq 0
, and the rightmost internal sum ranges over all ordered
decompositions
{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}}=r
and
{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{s}}=s
with positive
components. Then, the cochains

m_{n}^{\varphi^{*}M}\coloneqq(m_{p,q}^{\varphi^{*}M})_{p+1+q=n}\in\bigoplus_{p+1+q=n}\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{n-1,2-n}\!\left(A,M\right)

assemble into an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure

m^{\varphi^{*}M}\coloneqq(m_{1}^{\varphi^{*}M},m_{2}^{\varphi^{*}M},m_{3}^{\varphi^{*}M},\dots,m_{n}^{\varphi^{*}M},\dots)\in\prod_{n\geq 1}\operatorname{C}_{A^{e}}^{n-1,2-n}\!\left(A,M\right)

on
M
over
(A,m^{A})
.

Proof.

The proof is straightforward, but somewhat tedious, and is left to the
reader.
23
23

23

The proof can be streamlined by using the brace relation
recalled in
eq.

3.21
.

We only observe that the vertical degree of cochain of the form

{m_{r,s}^{M}}\left\{\varphi_{i_{1}},\dots,\varphi_{i_{r}},\mathrm{id}_{M},\varphi_{j_{1}},\dots,\varphi_{j_{s}}\right\}

is equal to the vertical degree of
m_{r,s}^{M}
since

\sum_{a=1}^{r}\lvert\varphi_{i_{a}}\rvert_{\mathrm{v}}=\sum_{a=1}^{r}(1-i_{a})=r-\sum_{a=1}^{r}i_{a}=0

and

\sum_{b=1}^{s}\lvert\varphi_{j_{b}}\rvert_{\mathrm{v}}=\sum_{b=1}^{s}(1-j_{b})=s-\sum_{b=1}^{s}j_{b}=0

by assumption.
∎

We remind the reader of the following elementary fact. Let
A
and
B
be
algebras and
M
a
B
-bimodule. Then, given a morphism of algebras

\varphi\colon A\to B
, there is a unique
A
-bimodule structure on
M
, denoted

\varphi^{*}M
, such that the map

\widetilde{\varphi}=\varphi\oplus\mathrm{id}_{M}\colon A\oplus\varphi^{*}M\longrightarrow B\oplus M

is a morphism of algebras where both source and target are endowed with the
corresponding square-zero product. Indeed, for
x,y\in A
and
m\in M
we must have

x\cdot m\cdot y=\widetilde{\varphi}(x\cdot m\cdot y)=\widetilde{\varphi}(x)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(m)\cdot\widetilde{\varphi}(y)=\varphi(x)\cdot m\cdot\varphi(y).

This observation admits the following generalisation to
A_{\infty}
-algebras and

A_{\infty}
-bimodules.

5.54
.

Let
(M,m^{M})
be an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
(B,m^{B})
. Then,

m^{\varphi^{*}M}
is the unique
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure on
M
over

(A,m^{A})
such that

\widetilde{\varphi}\coloneqq\varphi\oplus\mathrm{id}_{M}\colon(A\oplus M,m^{A\ltimes\varphi^{*}M})\longrightarrow(B\oplus M,m^{B\ltimes M})

is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras.

Proof.

Let
m
be an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure on
M
over
(A,m^{A})
.
Consider now the candidate map

\widetilde{\varphi}\coloneqq\varphi\oplus\mathrm{id}_{M}\colon(A\oplus M,m^{A}+m)\longrightarrow(B\oplus M,m^{B\ltimes M})

Keeping in mind that
\varphi
is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism of

A_{\infty}
-algebras by assumption and that
\widetilde{\varphi}_{0,0}=\mathrm{id}_{M}
, the

A_{\infty}
-morphism equation for
\widetilde{\varphi}
translates to the
requirement that, for

p,q\geq 0
and
n\coloneqq p+1+q
,

m_{p,q}={\widetilde{\varphi}_{0,0}}\left\{m_{p,q}\right\}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{r+1+s=k}\sum{m_{r,s}^{M}}\left\{\varphi_{i_{1}},\dots,\varphi_{i_{r}},\mathrm{id}_{M},\varphi_{j_{1}},\dots,\varphi_{j_{s}}\right\},

where the middle internal sum ranges over all ordered decompositions
r+1+s=n

with
r,s\geq 0
, and the rightmost internal sum ranges over all ordered
decompositions
{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}}=r
and
{j_{1}+\cdots+j_{s}}=s
with positive
components. Uniqueness is then clear, and existence is precisely the statement
of
5.53
.
∎

Proposition

5.54
admits the following
variants.

5.55
.

Let
(M,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
(A,m^{A})
, and
(N,m^{N})

and
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
(B,m^{B})
. Suppose given a cochain

\psi=(\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3},\dots,\psi_{n},\dots)

with

\psi_{n}=(\psi_{p,q}\colon A^{\otimes p}\otimes M\otimes A^{\otimes q}\longrightarrow N)_{p+1+q=n}.

Then,

\psi\colon(M,m^{M})\longrightarrow(N,m^{\varphi^{*}N})

is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules over
(A,m^{A})
if and only
if

\varphi\oplus\psi\colon(A\otimes M,m^{A\ltimes M})\longrightarrow(B\oplus N,m^{B\ltimes N})

is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras

Proof.

The claim follows by noticing that both statements correspond to the validity
of the exact same set of
A_{\infty}
-morphism equations.
∎

We now turn our attention to the compatibility between restriction of scalars
and the passage to shifts and linear duals of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules.

5.56
.

Let
(M,m^{M})
be an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
(B,m^{B})
. Then

(M(n),m^{\varphi^{*}(M)(n)})=(M(n),m^{\varphi^{*}(M(n))}),\qquad n\in\mathbb{Z},

and

(DM,m^{D(\varphi^{*}M)})=(DM,m^{\varphi^{*}DM}).

Proof.

Both claims are immediate from the definition, the key point is that, in an
expression of the form

{m_{r,s}}\left\{\varphi_{i_{1}},\dots,\varphi_{i_{r}},\mathrm{id},\varphi_{j_{1}},\dots,\varphi_{j_{s}}\right\},

we have
\sum_{a=1}^{r}\lvert\varphi_{i_{a}}\rvert_{\mathrm{v}}=0
and

\sum_{b=1}^{s}\lvert\varphi_{j_{b}}\rvert_{\mathrm{v}}=0
since, in the context of

5.53
, it is assumed that
i_{1}+\cdots+i_{r}=n

and
j_{1}+\cdots+j_{s}=s
. We leave the details to the reader.
∎

5.57
.

Let
(M,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
(A,m^{A})
, and
(N,m^{N})

an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
(B,m^{B})
. Suppose given a cochain

\psi=(\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3},\dots,\psi_{n},\dots),

with

\psi_{n}=(\psi_{p,q}\colon A^{\otimes p}\otimes M\otimes A^{\otimes q}\longrightarrow N)_{p+1+q=n},

that satisfies the equivalent conditions in
Proposition

5.55
.
Then,

\psi^{(n)}=(\psi_{1}^{(n)},\psi_{2}^{(n)},\psi_{3}^{(n)},\dots,\psi_{n}^{(n)},\dots),

also satisfies the equivalent conditions in
Proposition

5.55
.

Proof.

Indeed,
Proposition

5.47
shows that

\psi^{(n)}\colon(M(n),m^{M(n)})\longrightarrow(N(n),m^{\varphi^{*}(N)(n)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {prop:compatibility-restriction}}}{{=}}(N(n),m^{\varphi^{*}(N(n))})

is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules.
∎

5.58
.

Let
(M,m^{A})
be an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
(A,m^{A})
, and
(N,m^{N})

and
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
(B,m^{B})
. Suppose given a cochain

\psi=(\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3},\dots,\psi_{n},\dots),

with

\psi_{n}=(\psi_{p,q}\colon A^{\otimes p}\otimes N\otimes A^{\otimes q}\longrightarrow M)_{p+1+q=n},

such that

\psi\colon(N,m^{\varphi^{*}N})\longrightarrow(M,m^{\varphi^{*}M})

is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules over
(A,m^{A})
. Then,

\psi^{D}=(\psi_{1}^{D},\psi_{2}^{D},\psi_{3}^{D},\dots,\psi_{n}^{D},\dots),

satisfies the equivalent conditions in
Proposition

5.55
.

Proof.

Indeed,
Proposition

5.51
shows that

\psi^{D}\colon(DM,m^{DM})\longrightarrow(DN,m^{D\varphi^{*}(M)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref {prop:compatibility-restriction}}}{{=}}(DN,m^{\varphi^{*}(DN)})

is an
A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules.
∎

5.59
.

The previous discussion can be understood in conceptual terms as follows.
Consider the category
\operatorname{Bim}_{A_{\infty}}
of
all

A_{\infty}
-bimodules. Its
objects are pairs
((A,m^{A}),(M,m^{M}))
consisting of an

A_{\infty}
-algebra
(A,m^{A})
and an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule
(M,m^{M})
over
it. A morphism

((A,m^{A}),(M,m^{M}))\longrightarrow((B,m^{B}),(N,m^{N}))

between such pairs is itself a pair
(\varphi,\psi)
consisting of an

A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras

\varphi\colon(A,m^{A})\longrightarrow(B,m^{A})

and a
A_{\infty}
-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

\psi\colon(M,m^{M})\longrightarrow(N,m^{\varphi^{*}N})

over
(A,m^{A})
. Composition is induced by the composition law of

A_{\infty}
-morphisms (which we did not define in this article). There is also
a contravariant version
\operatorname{Bim}_{A_{\infty}}^{\vee}
in which
\psi
has instead the form

\psi\colon(M,m^{M})\longleftarrow(N,m^{\varphi^{*}N}).

Then, there is a globally-defined
n
-fold shift functor

\displaystyle[n]\colon\operatorname{Bim}_{A_{\infty}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\operatorname{Bim}_{A_{\infty}}

\displaystyle((A,m^{A}),(M,m^{M}))

\displaystyle\longmapsto((A,m^{A}),(M(n),m^{M(n)})),

defined using
5.45
and

Proposition

5.47
, and a globally-defined linear dual functor

\displaystyle D\colon\operatorname{Bim}_{A_{\infty}}^{\vee}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\operatorname{Bim}_{A_{\infty}}

\displaystyle((A,m^{A}),(M,m^{M}))

\displaystyle\longmapsto((A,m^{A}),(M,m^{DM})),

defined using
5.48
and

Proposition

5.51
. We have effectively verified that these
functors are well defined on objects and on morphisms, but we did not analyse
the compatibility with the composition law as this is not strictly necessary
for our purposes. As a side remark, we observe that
\operatorname{Bim}_{A_{\infty}}
and
\operatorname{Bim}_{A_{\infty}}^{\vee}

are the two variants of the Grothendieck construction of the
2
-functor

\operatorname{Alg}_{A_{\infty}}^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{CAT}_{\mathbf{k}},\qquad(A,m^{A})\longmapsto\operatorname{Bim}_{A_{\infty}}(A,m^{A}),

from the category of all
A_{\infty}
-algebras to the (very large)
2
-category
of large
\mathbf{k}
-categories, which associates to an
A_{\infty}
-algebra its
category of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules and to an
A_{\infty}
-morphism of

A_{\infty}
-algebras the corresponding restriction of scalars.

5.6.
Minimal models of dg algebras and dg bimodules

We now review the construction of minimal model for dg algebras. Since explicit
formulas for obtaining minimal models of dg bimodules do not seem to be
available in the literature, we explain how to use

Proposition

5.37
together with the well-known formulas for
the homotopy transfer of dg algebra structures to obtain them.
24
24

24

One can
also establish the existence of minimal models of dg bimodules using Quillen model
category techniques applied to the category of dg operads—we take this
alternative approach in
[
JM25
, Section 2]
.
We use the sign conventions in

[
Mar06
]
, as these are compatible our conventions for
A_{\infty}
-structures
(see the last part of
Remark

5.11
).

5.60
[
Kad82
]
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra, viewed as an

A_{\infty}
-algebra
(A,m^{A})
with vanishing higher operations
m_{n}^{A}
,

n\geq 3
. A
minimal model
for
A
is a triple

(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),m^{\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)},\varphi)
consisting of

•

a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra
(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),m^{\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)})

•

together with an
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-algebras

\varphi\colon(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),m^{\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}(A,m^{A})

whose linear part
\varphi_{1}\colon\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)\to A
is a
cocycle selection
map
, that is

\forall x\in\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),\qquad[\varphi_{1}(x)]=x.

Since we work over a ground field, minimal models for dg algebras (and even for

A_{\infty}
-algebras) always exist. The proof of this fact, known as the Homotopy
Transfer Theorem, goes back to Kadeishvili
[
Kad82
]
and there are beautiful
explicit formulas for the transferred
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure given in
terms of trees
[
Mer99
,
KS01
,
Mar04
,
Mar06
]
. We begin by recalling the
necessary terminology.

5.61
.

We define
planar binary (rooted) trees =(PBTs)
recursively as follows:
The empty set is a PBT, which we denote by
()
. If
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are PBTs,
then

T\coloneqq{T_{1}}\vee{T_{2}}\coloneqq(T_{1},T_{2})

is a PBT, with the
left subtree

L(T)\coloneqq T_{1}
and the
right
subtree
R(T)\coloneqq T_{2}
; by convention, the empty PBT does not have
(proper) subtrees. Given a PBT, say
T
, its
arity (=number of leaves)

is defined recursively as

a(T)\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&T=()\\
a(L(T))+a(R(T))&T\neq(),\end{cases}

and, when
T\neq()
, its
signature
is

\sigma(T)\coloneqq a(L(T))(a(R)+1).

We denote the set of PBTs of arity
n
by
\operatorname{PBT}_{n}
.

5.62
.

Of course, PBTs have a natural pictorial presentation, with the tree

{()}\vee{()}
depicted as

and with a PBT with at least one non-empty subtree depicted by ‘grafting’ its
left and right subtrees onto the left and the right leaves of the above PBT,
respectively. For example, the PBT
{({()}\vee{()})}\vee{()}
is depicted
as

r
3
2
1
e

et cetera
. We could give rigorous definitions of ‘leaves’, ‘internal
vertices’, ‘internal edges’ and ‘root vertex’ of a PBT, as well as of the
canonical linear order on the leaves; since these concepts are quite
intuitive, we do not do so here and trust that the reader will nonetheless be
able to follow the forthcoming discussion. For example, the above tree has
three leaves (no pun intended) ordered
1<2<3
, a single internal (unlabelled)
vertex, one internal edge
e
and the root vertex
r
. It is elementary to
verify that the number of internal edges in a non-empty PBT, say
T
, is

a(T)-2
(compare with the degree of an operation in an
A_{\infty}
-algebra
structure).

5.63
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra with binary operation
m_{2}^{A}
. Since we work over a
field, we can find a
strong deformation retraction
of
A
onto
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
,
that is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

of homogeneous morphisms of graded vector spaces of degrees

|i|=0,\quad|p|=0,\quad|h|=-1,

such that
pi=\mathrm{id}_{\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)}
and which also satisfy the following equations:

\displaystyle\partial(i)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\partial(p)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\partial(h)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{id}_{V}-ip,

\displaystyle ph

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle hi

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle h^{2}

\displaystyle=0,

see for example
[
Val14
, Section 1.4]
. The last three equations are called

side conditions
(
[
Mar06
, Remark 4]
) and can be always achieved
after appropriately modifying the morphisms
i
,
p
, and
h
.
Following
[
Mar06
]
, we define a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra structure on

\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
with operations

m_{n}^{\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)}\coloneqq\sum_{T\in\operatorname{PBT}_{n}}p^{A}\bullet_{1}\left({\widetilde{m}_{T}^{A}}\left\{i^{A},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n}}{{\dots}},i^{A}\right\}\right),\qquad n\geq 2,

where the auxiliary degree
2-n
operations

\widetilde{m}_{T}^{A}\colon A^{\otimes n}\longrightarrow A

are defined recursively as follows:

\widetilde{m}_{T}^{A}\coloneqq\begin{cases}m_{2}^{A}&L(T)=(),\ R(T)=()\\
m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{1}(h^{A}\bullet_{1}\widetilde{m}_{L(T)}^{A})&L(T)\neq(),\ R(T)=()\\
m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{2}(h^{A}\bullet_{1}\widetilde{m}_{R(T)}^{A})&L(T)=(),\ R(T)\neq()\\
(m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{1}(h^{A}\bullet_{1}\widetilde{m}_{L(T)}^{A}))\bullet_{a(L(T))+1}(h^{A}\bullet_{1}\widetilde{m}_{R(T)}^{A})&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

A straightforward computation using
eq.

3.28
shows
that, in all four cases,

\displaystyle(p\bullet_{1}\left({\widetilde{m}_{T}^{A}}\left\{i,\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n}}{{\dots}},i\right\}\right))=(-1)^{\sigma(T)}p\circ m_{2}^{A}\circ((h^{A}\circ\widetilde{m}_{L(T)}^{A})\otimes(h^{A}\circ\widetilde{m}_{R(T)}^{A}))\circ i^{\otimes n},

where we set
\widetilde{m}_{T}^{A}\coloneqq\mathrm{id}_{A}
if
T=()
. Let us show this in
the fourth case, which is the most complicated (but still trivial). Firstly,
it follows immediately from
eq.

3.28
that post
composition with a unary operation does not contribute any signs, and neither
does the pre-composition with unary operations of (vertical) degree
0
.
Secondly, it also turns out that the infinitesimal composition

m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{1}(h^{A}\bullet_{1}\widetilde{m}_{L(T)}^{A})

also has no contribution:

(a(L(T))-1)(2-1)+(1-a(L(T)))(2-1)=0,

where we use that
h^{A}\bullet_{1}\widetilde{m}_{L(T)}^{A}
has degree

(2-a(L(T)))-1=1-a(L(T))
. Finally, pre-composition with

h^{A}\bullet_{1}\widetilde{m}_{R(T)}^{A}
in position
a(L(T)+1)
contributes the sign
determined by the parity of the expression

\Big(a(R)-1\Big)\underbrace{\Big(a(L(T))+1-(a(L(T))+1)\Big)}_{=0}\\
+\Big(1-a(R(T))\Big)\Big(a(L(T))+1-1\Big)=(1-a(R(T)))a(L(T)),

which is equal to the signature
\sigma(T)
modulo
2
.

5.64
.

At this juncture is worth noting that, taken modulo
2
, the signature of a
PBT is precisely the sign associated to the root vertex in

[
Mar06
, Section 4]
, so that our sign conventions agree with those in

op. cit.
We have chosen the above presentation as it relies only on our
standing sign conventions concerning infinitesimal compositions.

5.65
.

5.63
is better visualised using PBTs that are decorated
by the data of the chosen strong deformation retraction as follows: Leaves are
decorated with
i^{A}
, internal edges are decorated with
h^{A}
, and internal
vertices are understood as the application of the binary operation

m_{2}^{A}
. Finally, the root vertex is decorated by
p^{A}
. The tree is
then read as a flowchart for describing the corresponding summand of the
operation. For example, the decorated PBT

p^{A}
i^{A}
i^{A}
i^{A}
h^{A}

describes the operation

p^{A}\circ(m_{2}^{A}\bullet_{1}(h^{A}\bullet_{1}m_{2}^{A}))\circ(i^{A}\otimes i^{A}\otimes i^{A}).

5.66
[
Mar06
]
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra. The system of operations
m^{\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)}
described in

5.63
endows the cohomology
\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)
of
A
with the
structure of a minimal
A_{\infty}
-algebra. Moreover, the morphisms
i
and
p

extend to mutually homotopy-inverse
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphisms, so that

(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),m^{\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right)})
yields a minimal model for
A
.

We now explain how to leverage
5.63
to construct minimal
models of dg bimodules.

5.67
.

Let
A
be a dg algebra and
M
a dg
A
-bimodule. A
minimal model
of
the pair
(A,M)
consists of

•

a minimal model
(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),m^{A},\varphi)
of
A
,

•

a minimal
A_{\infty}
-bimodule
(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right),m^{\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right)})
over

(\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(A\right),m^{A},\varphi)
,

•

together with a cochain

\psi=(\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3},\dots,\psi_{n},\dots)

in

\prod_{n\geq 1}\bigoplus_{p+1+q=n}\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{k}}\!\left(A^{\otimes p}\otimes\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right)\otimes A^{\otimes q},M\right)

whose linear part
\psi_{1}\colon\operatorname{H}^{\bullet}\!\left(M\right)\to M
is a cocycle selection map and
such that the component-wise direct sum
